I will swear it was not later than nine o'clock, and I believe it was not so late as nine—I do not recollect the exact hour, I believe it was very little after eight o'clock—I was in and out after that two or three times—I first went to the hotel after I left the canvass, I believe a little after eight o'clock—I cannot recollect the exact time—Mr. Badnall was speaking at the
Roebuck—I believe it was then nine o'clock, and it was a considerable time before that that I went to the Castle.
Q. When did you first go to the Castle, after half-past six o'clock? A. bout eight o'clock—I cannot speak positively within half an hour—I can within an hour, to the best of my recollection—I should have no hesitation in saying that it was not later than half-past eight o'clock—Mr. Miller was not with me at the Roebuck and heard Mr. Badnall—I was not at the Roebuck—I passed by it—not when he was speaking—I was at distance—I was certainly within sight and hearing of Mr. Badnall—I do not know that I could understand the words, but I could hear the the sound—I will swear Mr. Miller was not with me—I have no recollection of being at the Royal Express beer-house with Mr. Miller—I will swear I was not at half-past eight o'clock—I do not think I was at eight o'clock—I have no recollection of being there at all—I really cannot say that I was there with Mr. Miller as late as eight o'clock—I will not swear that I was not, because I cannot recollect every where I went—I was in every house in the town during the election—I do not know Boot, the tenant of that house—I cannot say whether I went with Mr. Miller to this house, and there canvassed Boot for his vote—I cannot say whether I did or not—I have no recollection of it—that house is in Lower-street-if I was in that house I must have gone into Lower-street—I cannot say the exact course I took now—I told you before I cannot swear to every abode I was in daring the election—I went back to the Castle that evening, but whether I went up Church-lane I cannot say—I did not, on my solemn oath, go back with Mr. Miller to the Castle that evening—I cannot recollect whether I was in Church-lane or not—I went to the Castle again—it might be eleven or twelve o'clock before I left the Castle that night—some of the committee were there that night—I cannot say it was a formal meeting.
Q. Was Mr. Miller in the habit of going to and fro to the Committee? A. I do not recollect seeing Mr. Miller in the committee-room, but when the committee was formed he was there—I never saw him there when any one but the committee was present—I do not recollect his being there at all—he did not walk arm-in-arm with any person into the Castle on that night after half-past eight o'clock, to my knowledge—I went out with Mr. Miller to canvass—I have some recollection of being in Paradise-street—it is impossible for me to tell who I canvassed that night—I went to a person's house, I believe, of the name of Watkins—Mr. Miller was there—we went together with a whole party of gentlemen—Mr. De Horsey, and the whole party—I never saw Mr. Miller at the hotel after that canvass—it is very improbable he would be there—I do not know that I went back with Mr. De Horsey after the canvass—Mr. De Horsey went to the hotel, because it was his house—I might be in his company after the canvass, but I cannot recollect that he was arm-in-arm with any one—he certainly might have been.
Q. On your solemn oath, after you went into the hotel, and canvassed that evening, did not Mr. Miller address a very few words to the crowd from the balcony or window? A. The understanding was that no speeches were to be made that evening—Mr. Miller did not address a few words after my return from the canvass, stating that he was fatigued, and wishing them good night, or ask them to be early at the poll on Monday morning—I have no recollection of having heard him—I have no recollection of any one having done so—I feel quite certain that no one did address the crowd
while I was there that night—I swear that I never heard it—I positively swear no one did so in my presence—I was about, and if they had I mad have heard them.
MR. KELLY. Q. I think you say it was about half-past six o'clock that you left the Castle hotel to canvass with Mr. Miller, and others? A. It was—I cannot say exactly how long Mr. Miller remained canvassing in my company—I have no recollection of seeing Mr. Miller that evening after eight o'clock, or about that time—he continued canvassing with me, to the best of my recollection, till about eight o'clock—I shortly afterwards returned to the Castle—Mr. Miller was not with me—I did not set eyes on Mr. Miller for the rest of that night.
MARY MASON . I am the wife of Mr. Mason, of Newcastle. I remember the last general election in July—I had been absent from home in the Isle of Man—I returned home on the Thursday before the nomination—Mr. Miller resided at our house, from the time of my return till the election had taken place—he slept there every night—he dined there on the Friday and the Sunday, and the Monday too—he did not dine there on the Saturday—I remember Saturday, the nomination day—he left home that day very early in the morning, as soon as he had his breakfast—I was at home during the evening of that Saturday—Mr. Miller came home that evening after eight o'clock, but not half-past eight o'clock—I first received him in the lobby of the house, he then went into the drawing-room—it was not half-past eight o'clock—he took tea with me in the drawing-room, and passed the remainder of the evening with me—he did not leave the house again before he went to bed-when he went to bed it was after ten o'clock, but it could not be more than half-past ten o'clock—he could not have left the house so as to be at the Castle between nine and ten o'clock, and come back, without my knowing it—I do not remember when my husband came home that night—I was gone to bed—I went to bed after Mr. Miller.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. I suppose you looked at your watch or clock? A. I looked at the clock just after it struck eight—it had finished striking eight—Mr. Miller was not come in then—I felt hungry, having had no dinner that day, and ordered my servants to get me a cup of tea, and I would have some more with Mr. Miller when he came in, and while I was taking the first cup of tea he came in—I cannot, say whether he dined at our house on the Thursday—I was not at home—he dined at our house on Friday, with several other friends, and on Sunday he dined at our house—he went out to church on Sunday morning, and returned and took a lunch, and then went into the pottery—we got our dinners—Mr. Miller told us not to wait, and we had just finished dinner when he came back—I never heard it said at all that Mr. Miller and my husband had been bribing—I never heard from my husband that he had been bribing voters at former elections—I cannot tell how far our house in from the Castle hotel, but I can walk it in about seven minutes—I cannot walk it in one or two—I can walk it in five—sometimes I walk it in seven sometimes in five—I cannot tell whether it is fifty yards from our house—it is more than twenty—we do not live in a street, we live on the London-road, and the Castle is in High-street—I mean to swear Mr. Miller was is our house at half-past eight o'clock, and was never out of my sighs till half-past ten o'clock—it was not half-past ten o'clock when he went to feed—I swear he was in our house at half-past eight o'clock, and never left the room till he went to bed-ours is a private house—there are two outlets to it.
MR. KELLY. Q. When he left to go to bed, did you see which way he went? A. I went out of the room with him, and saw him ascend the stairs, with the candle in his hand.
COURT. Q. When was your attention first called to this circumstance? After they had sworn in London—I was asked about it by Mr. Hyatt, Mr. Miller's attorney.
MR. KELLY. Q. After you heard something had been sworn to in the committee, did they send down for you, and bring you up as a witness before the Committee? A. Yes.
EDWARD THOMPSON . I am a solicitor, living in Stone-buildings, Lincoln's Inn. I was acting for Mr. De Horsey at the last Newcastle election, is a friend—I arrived at Newcastle on Saturday, the nomination day, and went to the Castle—I dined there—Mr. Miller dined with me—we sat down about a Quarter or ten minutes after five o'clock—Mr. Miller and Mr. De Horsey went out to finish their canvass that evening—they left the tables of the best of my recollection, about half-past six o'clock—I remained at the Castle that evening—there were no speeches whatever made that evening from the Castle windows—Mr. De Horsey returned to the Castle that evening, I should say from half-past eight to nine o'clock, about half-past eight o'clock—Mr. Miller did not return with him—I did not see Mr. Miller again at the Castle that night, after he left at half-past six o'clock.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. You are Mr. De Horsey's attorney? A. Yes, but did not go down professionally—I do not know that I have ever said that Mr. De Horsey did not come till half-past nine o'clock—I said, before the Committee of the House of Commons, that I could not say positively for half an hour—I said there what I say now—I should say it was from half-past eight to nine o'clock—I cannot undertaken swear that it was before half-past nine o'clock—I know the bed-room up stairs—I was going up and down in different rooms that evening occasionally—I saw Mr. Mason in a bed-room that evening—I cannot say how often—I should think three or four times—that bed-room was up stairs—I cannot say whether it was on the left or right-hand side—he was speaking with other gentlemen—I went in out of curiosity—I do not know who the gentlemen were—I cannot tell whether they were voters or not—I am a perfect stranger to Newcastle—I cannot say whether he was speaking to persons in the bed-room every time I went up—there was a general conversation going on—I cannot recollect whether he was speaking among the rest—I cannot remember whether he was writing on a table—I was not there as one of the committee—I went there out of curiosity—I think there were no persons' names called over when I was going up and down stairs-to the best of my recollection, there was not—I think I can positively say there was not when I was in the room—I cannot recollect how late it was when I went into the bed-room, and saw Mr. Mason—I did not go to bed that night till one, or two, or half-past two o'clock—I do not know whether there was a good deal of business to do—I was no part of the committee—I was sitting up in the house—I went there as a friend—I think I was in Mr. De Horsey's sitting-room till late in the evening, with Mr. De Horsey—there were some other gentlemen there occasionally—I do not think Mr. Mason was there—I do not think I saw him in the bed-room as late as eleven o'clock at night—I think I walked out of the house for ten minutes after dinner—I should say that was about eight o'clock—I can positively say it was about eight o'clock—I
cannot say for ten minutes-and then I was only outside the door, in the immediate neighbourhood of the Castle—on my honour, I cannot say where Mr. Mason was at that time—I cannot say whether he was in the house or not—I do not remember whether I left him in the hotel—I did not speak to him as I went out, to my recollection—I do not remember seeing him in the street—I cannot swear that I did not speak is him in the street, but I do not remember seeing him out—I did not go near the Roebuck, where Mr. Badnall was, to my recollection—I heard some speaking when I went out in the front—I saw a crowd opposite the Roebuck, and heard a voice very indistinctly, but did not go near it—I do not know the room called by Mr. Mason the committee-room—I dinner with the gentlemen at that house that Saturday.
Q. Was it the front-room, or the back-room? A. That will enable was to explain a little mistake I made before the Committee—it was the back-room, and neither on the ground-floor, nor the first-floor—I was pressed very closely before the Committee, and said I believed it was on the ground floor—it now turns out to be neither one nor the other, it was on the landing—the ten minutes was the only time I went out, to the best of my recollection—I only went in and out of the doors—I did not go on any rambles, to the best of my recollection—I will swear I did not—I went in and out at the door occasionally.
Q. Have you not sworn that in the course of your rambles you saw Mr. Miller go away? A. To the best of my recollection I have not—I will not undertake to say I have not—I did not go out more than once that night, and that was just outside the door, and back again—I resided in the Castle—I cannot say I was in the house the whole evening—I went just outside the door, and back again.
Q. Did you go out, except for the ten minutes you have named? A. I went outside the door, but not to leave the Castle—I may have gone out—I may have gone out as many as half a dozen times, I cannot say—I went just in front of the Castle, and back-to the best of my recollection I did not go to anybody's house—I think I can swear I did not—I do not remember going into anybody's house—I can swear, to the best of my recollection, I did not—I do say positively I did not go into anybody's house.
Q. Did you converse with anybody in the street, after you had been out for the ten minutes you speak of? A. I really cannot tell—I might merely ask a question, but I do not remember it—I really think I was not out for about ten minutes, more than once or twice; if I was, it was merely in front of the Castle—I do not think I was out, more than once for a the, longer than ten minutes—I really cannot remember being out three time for ten minutes—I will swear I was not away from the front of the Castle more than ten minutes, nor for five minutes, besides the time I have named—I merely went out to get the air, to lounge about-is it likely I should remain, from half-past six to two o'clock, in one room?—Mr. De Horsey had a sitting-room at the Castle—that was open to persons coming there on the subject of the election—I really cannot say whether the bed-room was in a private place, and used for private purposes—I was no part of the committee—I have not stated on oath, that I am aware of, that the room was in a private place, and used for private purposes-if I did make that statement, I am not at all connected with the committee—there were several gentlemen in the room—I went in from curiosity, I did not know the nature of the room.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Do you know on what floor the bed-room was, is which you saw Mr. Mason? A. The second floor—I did not see Mr. Miller
in any bed-room, or in any room in the house, after half-past six o'clock that night.
HENRY BAGULEY . I am servant to Mr. Mason, of Newcastle, and have been so going on for thirteen years. I remember the last election for that borough—the nomination-day was on a Saturday—on that day I attended at the Castle hotel to assist at the dinner—my master, Mr. Mason, dined there with Mr. Miller, and some other gentlemen—they dined, it might be, about five o'clock—I assisted in waiting at dinner—the party broke up about six o'clock, I think—I saw my master go out of the hotel, and Mr. Miller also—that was about six o'clock—I remained about an hour after they left, and then I went—I did not go straight home—I remained about the town about three quarters of an hour—I arrived at my master's house about eight o'clock, or a few minutes to eight—Mr. Miller was not in the house when I got there—he came in in about a quarter of an hour, or twenty minutes after—I did not let him in—Mrs. Mason was at home when Mr. Miller came in—he went into the drawing-room—Mrs. Mason was there—he took tea—I waited at tea—Mr. Miller and Mrs. Mason took tea together—Mr. Miller did not leave the house of Mr. Mason again that night—I was at home all the evening after I got home—Mr. Miller went bed about ten o'clock that night, or a few minutes after—I took tome not water to him in his bed-room, it might be ten minutes after ten o'clock.
Cross-examined by MR. JOKES. Q. Are you a domestic servant; a footman to Mr. Mason? A. No—I live in the house—I am a miller—Mr. Mason is a miller as well as a hat-maker—I eat in the house—I had been assisting in waiting at that time—that is not what I do generally—I assist when there are parties—there are two female servants—they were at home.
Q. What had you to go by as to the time that night? A. There was a clock in the kitchen—I was brought up from Newcastle to give evidence before the Committee of the House of Commons, but was not examined.
EMMA HARVEY . I was formerly in Mr. Mason's service in Newcastle, but have left now. I was there at the time of the election—I remember Mrs. Mason coming home from the Isle of Man—it was on the Thursday of the election—Mr. Miller was living at our house at the time, and he dined there on the Friday, the day after—I remember the Saturday, the day after that—he did not dine there that day—I was in the house during that Saturday evening—I remember Mr. Miller coming home about half-past eight o'clock—it was quite light then—he took tea with Mrs. Mason when he came into the drawing-room—he did not go out again that night—he went to bed about eleven o'clock.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Were you brought to town to give evidence before the Committee of the House of Commons? A. Yes—I was not examined—I cannot exactly say the day of the month I left Mr. Mason's service—it was about six weeks since—I left because I did not much like Mr. Mason's—there was no other reason—I had no quarrel or misunderstanding—I thought to better myself.
Q. When were you first spoken to about this case, and by whom? A. By Mr. Hyatt, Mr. Miller's lawyer, about a fortnight ago—that was not the first time I was spoken to about it—I was spoken to before I left Mr. Mason's, by Mr. Hyatt—I think that is about eight weeks ago—Mr. Mason was not with Mr. Hyatt when he spoke to me—Mr. Hyatt was by himself—I
gave my statement to him—he spoke to me on the Friday, and I came to London on the Monday—I staid in London till the following Saturday night—Mr. Mason did not come up with me, he was in London—I went to where he was in London, and after that went back into the country—I gave notice to leave about a fortnight after that—I gave a month's noticed Mr. Mason—I left because I thought I could better myself—I had a place is view at Mr. Henry Hall's, a timber-merchant, of Newcastle—I had no misunderstanding at all with my master or mistress—it was not from away thing about this election that I left—I swear that the election was not mentioned—I swear positively nothing passed on the subject—it was not mentioned by them—I did not object to come to London—I was quite willing to come.
Q. What other servants were in the house besides you? A. Henry Baguley and Jane Burn-nobody else—there was no other female service in the house at the time—I am quite sure of that; nor any male beside Henry—they paid my fare up and down to London, and something else—I do not know how much exactly—I think I received five pounds—Mr. Murray paid it to me in London—he did not tell me whose money it was-Jane Burn did not come to London.
MR. KELLY. Q. Did you first give notice of your intention of going away from Mr. Mason's before you came to London or after; try and recollect? A. I gave notice before I came up to London, about a fortnight before—I had no idea at that time that I was going to be sent for as a witness to London—I first heard I was wanted in London on a Friday and I left Newcastle to come to London on the Monday—I returned on the Saturday—the five pounds were given me for coming up to London, including coach-hire and expense—it was to pay the coach-hire and expenses-Newcastle is one hundred and fifty miles from London-Jane Burn is not constantly in the house—she is occasionally there.
COURT. Q. Did you receive five pounds, or did you pay your coach-hire and expenses out of it? A. I paid my coach-hire out of the five pounds.
PHILLIP PETTIT . I am servant to Mr. Miller. I went down with him to the Newcastle election—I remember Saturday, the day of the nomination—I attended at dinner, at the Castle, that day—I remember my master Mr. De Horsey, and some others, going out soon after dinner—I think it was about twenty minutes past six o'clock—I went into the large room after my master left—it is called the speaking-room—I was there about seven o'clock, and remained there till half-past ten o'clock—there were no speeches made out of the windows of that room, or from that room, from seven o'clock till half-past ten o'clock that night—I expected my master back when he left, but he did not return—I know Thomas Baddeley—I saw him there that night, standing on the stairs.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Why did yon expect your master back that evening? A. Because he had not had tea, and I expected him back to take tea there, as he had dined there—he did not tell me he should come back, but I thought it my duty to stop—I am an in-door servant—I act as valet, or footman—I do not wear a livery—I did not go about with my master canvassing—I have gone out occasionally, but not to have any interest in it—I have heard him ask the poor voters for their votes.
Q. Have you heard them snub him, and abuse him, and say they would not vote for him? A. Never, to the best of my knowledge—I never, to
the best of my knowledge, heard them tell him the old account was not settled—I will swear I do not recollect it—to the best of my recollection, I never heard it—I will not swear I never heard it, but I do not remember it—I shall not swear I have not heard it a dozen times, because I do not remember any thing about it, not hearing such language repeated—I never heard the poor voters brow-beat him—I never heard them say they would not vote for him till the old account was settled—I do not remember any such expressions—I will swear that I never heard it said.
Q. Tell us any thing you may have heard from them? A. I object to such a question as that, because I do not understand it—to the best of my recollection, I never heard the poor voters refuse to vote for him, for that reason—I have heard as many as a hundred say, if he came twenty times they would always vote for him-to the best of. my recollection, I never heard them refuse to vote for him—they said they would vote for him, because they liked the man, or gentleman, but man was the common phrase I heard—I have been a year and nine months in Mr. Miller's service—I have been at three elections—I have gone about a dozen yards with Mr. Mason, perhaps—I never saw the money going about—I took money of my own down to the election.
Q. Did you have a portmanteau of sovereigns that went to Liverpool instead of Newcastle? A. No—we have had no such thing—we have had boxes which I travel with—I never knew there were any sovereigns in them—I never knew or heard of a box of sovereigns going to Liverpool instead of Newcastle.
THOMAS BASTERFIELD . I am servant to Mr. De Horsey, M. P. for Newcastle. I was down there with him, at the last election—I remember the day of the nomination—I assisted in waiting at dinner, at the Castle hotel that day—my master dined there—the dinner was served up about five o'clock, in a large room, at the back of the house, and was over about half-past six o'clock—Mr. Miller and Mr. De Horsey, and most of the gentlemen left the hotel at that time—I remained in the hotel after that—I know the room in front of the house from which the speeches are made—I went into that room—it is on the first floor—I remained there from seven o'clock until ten o'clock—I did not see Mr. Miller at the hotel after half-past six o'clock—I was there all the time—there were no speeches made from the window that day.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Were you in the room all the time? A. I never left it—Mr. De Horsey is quite well, and in London.
THOMAS BADDELEY . I am in the employ of Mr. Mason, in his hatmanufactory. I was at Newcastle at the time of the last election, in July—I remember being at the Castle on the Saturday, the day of the nomination—the gentlemen had begun dinner when I went, but I was there before they had done—I got there between five and six o'clock at night—after dinner, I remember Mr. Miller and the rest leaving the Castle—that was between six and seven o'clock at night—I remained at the Castle till eleven o'clock—Mr. Miller never returned to the Castle at all that night—there were no speeches made from the room that evening.
Q. Were you at any time that evening standing in the passage or lobby of the house? A. I was on the top of the stairs on the lobby—I recollect Gallimore coming into the Castle between nine and ten o'clock that night-Plant and Wall were with him—I was at the top of the stairs at that time—they came up to the top of the stairs—they were quite in liquor—they
inquired for Mr. Miller and for Mr. Mason—I told them Mr. Mason was gone down stairs, and told them that Mr. Miller left between six and seven o'clock that night, and I had never seen him since-when I told that this, they rushed straight past me, up two steps, into another lobby, and were haggling with a person, named Rhodes, in the lobby—I did not hear any thing which either of them said, only swearing, but nothing that I could collect what they said—I cannot say that I heard them speak to anybody besides Rhodes—I did not hear them ask for any thing, to my knowledge—there was a vast many up the stairs, and a constable was sent for to clear the stairs, and he cleared them out of the hotel.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. How long have you been in Mr. Mason's service? A. Several years, regularly in his employ-old Mr. Mason went out with Mr. Miller—the Mr. Mason who has been examined is his son—he did not go out with him—Mr. Miller went out between six and seven o'clock—I cannot tell exactly when Mr. Mason went out—it might be an hour after, or it might be two—the gentlemen dined in a room at the back of the house—they did not go down into the room where the speeches are made—I have been examined before about this.
Q. Did you say this, "The room I speak of is the room out of the windows of which the gentlemen speak; that is the room in which the gentlemen dined that day?" A. Not to my knowledge, I did not—I did not swear that the gentlemen dined in the front room, not to my recollection—I did not say it was the room out of which I saw the dishes brought, not to my knowledge—that must be a mistake somehow—I did not say so, to my recollection.
Q. Were you not repeatedly warned to mind what you said, and persist in saying it was the front room, from the windows of which the speeches are made, that they dined in on the Saturday? A. I cannot say—I was repeatedly warned, and I said the dining-room was the room the gentlemen made a regular rule of speaking from, but not the room they dined in that day—I did not, to my recollection, say that I saw the dishes brought out of the room from the windows of which the gentlemen spoke—I cannot swear it.
Q. What were you up on the stairs to watch for? A. I was put there by Mr. Cork, the landlord of the inn, if anybody came to inquire for gentlemen, to say they were wanted—he placed me on the top of the stairs, to tell any gentleman who was inquired for that he was wanted—I know one bed-room there—I do not know that Mr. Mason used any bed-room that night.
Q. If he used a bed-room on the first floor on the left hand as late as eleven o'clock that night, must not you have seen him if he went in and out? A. There is one room he might have gone into which I could not see—that is where Mr. De Horsey's sitting-room was—I did not see Mr. Mason in any bed-room that night—I saw him several times when he returned from canvassing—I did not see him go into the bed-room—he went into the front speaking-room, and he went into Mr. De Horsey's sitting-room—the last time I saw him that night was about nine o'clock—he went then into what I call the dining-room on the left hand, what I call the speaking-room—I thought he went down stairs, because I told Gallimore, Plant, and Wall that he was gone down stairs—the last time I saw him he went into the dining-room, but I may have been walking up and down the lobby and not see him leave-if I had been on the top of the
stairs I should have seen him come out, but I did not always stand in one posture-many folks ran up and down stairs while I was standing there—there were not many people inquiring for persons that night—I cannot tell whether there was twenty—I did not take particular notice—there were not forty, nor twenty, hut I cannot tell, I did not put them down-Gallimore plant, and Wall inquired for Mr. Mason—I do not recollect any one else inquiring for him—I was not asked by many people for Mr. Mason—I do not live in the house with Mr. Mason—I am a weekly servant, and am paid by the week—I have my wages the same as a working man—I do not follow any business but a hatter.
Q. Have you been paid for coming up before the Committee? A. Yes—I cannot tell you how much—I cannot tell whether it is 7 l. 8 l., of 9 l., or what it is—I am sure I cannot tell how long I was in town—it was somewhere about nine or ten days, I believe—I came up by the coach—I paid my own expenses up—Mr. Murray paid them down—I cannot tell what I received for myself besides my expenses up and down—I was boarded at a public-house, and did not pay—we received our expenses separately-every witness received their own.
Q. Did you receive the money which was paid you, besides what you were paid for coming up and down? A. Yes, I believe I received it on Saturday night—I did not go back with Emma Harvey—I was on the top of the coach on Sunday—I cannot tell what day of the week I came up—I was paid in sovereigns—I cannot tell how many I had—it was not twenty, nor fifteen, nor twelve, nor ten—I swear I had not ten—I tell you I had eight or nine—I cannot tell which—I got no wages for the time I was in London—I earn 7 s. 6 d. a day when I work—I work regularly for Mr. Mason—I have earned 7 s. 6 d. a day many times—the 8 l. or 9 l. was to include my expenses down, and while I—was here—I mean loss of time—the eating and drinking was paid for.
Q. Are you a voter? A. Yes, a freeman—I voted at the last election for Mr. Miller and Mr. De Horsey—I did not vote for them before, because Mr. De Horsey never came before—I voted for Mr. Miller three times be fore—I never received a farthing for my vote in my life—I have never been in trouble in my life—I am a married man—my wife has not had any money, to my knowledge—I never found her in possession of money I was not aware of—I was canvassed by Mr. Miller at this election—I was not paid for standing at the top of the stairs—Mr. Cork placed us there—he asked me—I do not know how many voters Mr. Mason has in his employment—it ebbs and flows—sometimes he has between twenty and forty—I have known him to have one or two hundred freemen.
Q. Did you see 5 l. paid to the freemen? A. Is that a fair question to ask?
MR. KELLY. Q. you say you never received any money at all for your vote at any time? A. At no time—I received the 8 l. or 9 l. from the solicitor when I went down—I do not know the precise sum, whether it was 7 l., 8 l., or 9 l.—I arrived in town on Thursday, and remained till the following Saturday week—I have a wife and family—I paid my coach hire up to London myself—it cost me between 2 l. and 3 l.—I was in town nearly a fortnight—I paid ray coach hire down, out of the money Mr. Murray gave me—the rest I had for loss of time—I am quite sure, after Mr. Miller left the Castle, I never saw him there again that night.
JURY. Q. Is it usual to put an elector as a door-keeper? A. Yes; have seen it many times.
JAMES DITCHFLBLD . I am superintendent of the horse establishment at the Birmingham and Grand Junction Railway. I was at Newcastle at the last election—I was living at Mr. Cork's yard, at the Castle hotel—I was head ostler there—I remember the Saturday, the day of the nomination, perfectly well—I remember seeing Mr. Miller and Mr. De Horses there that day—they dined there—I assisted in carrying up and waiting at dinner—Mr. Miller, Mr. De Horsey, and others, went away after dinner—I was placed that evening at the bottom of the stairs—I took my station there about seven o'clock, and remained there till past twelve o'clock, nearly-Baddely and Down were placed at the top of the stairs—I am sure Baddely was there—I did not leave the bottom of; the stairs from seven till a little after twelve o'clock—Mr. Miller left dinner at half-past six o'clock, and I never saw him afterwards—I being at the bottom of the stairs, he could not have gone up without my seeing him-at ten o'clock several persons came—there was Wall, and several more that I do not know—I have often seen the defendant Gallimore—I really cannot say whether he was one of the persons—they were all tipsy-a constable was called, and one came and took Wall out-no speeches were made that night out of the window of the inn—there were many hundreds of persons in front of the Castle from seven to eight o'clock.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Do you remember the canvassers coming back? A. Yes, about ten o'clock—there are two staircases to the house—I am not a voter—I am sorry to say, I was not paid for waiting at the bottom of the stairs—my master, Mr. Cork, put me they—I was to keep the rabble from going up stairs—I was placed at the principal staircase, where all the people went up—the back stairs, are within sight of the front—I cannot say positively whether any one was placed at the foot of the back stairs.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Do both the stairs come on the same landing? A. Yes.
Q. Have you any reason for particularly remembering that there were no speeches made out of the windows that night? A. Many people said Mr. Badnall had given the members enough that night without any speeches—that was the remark of the crowd.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Were you inside the house, or outside? A. Inside the house.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did Mr. De Horsey return alone? A. I believe he did.
MR. CLAJRKSON. Q. Did you see him? A. I saw him and spoke to him-when I spoke to him he was alone—I cannot say whether five or six people came in with him; there were scores in the house—the crowd staid two hours before the hotel door—I heard them cry out "The canvassers are coming"—that was an hour before Mr. De Horsey returned—Mr. De Horsey's servant was in the house all the time he was gone.
FRANCIS STANYER . I am a solicitor at Newcastle. I was out can vassing on the nomination day for that borough—I afterwards dined at the Castle hotel with Mr. Miller and others, about six o'clock I think-no, I think not so late as that, it might be about five, but I cannot speak positively—I recollect it was not so late, or dinner could not have, been over at the time I got to my own house, which was about half-past six o'clock—I should say that we sat down to dinner at a very little after five o'clock—they
all left the hotel at the time I did, except about five—Mr. Miller and Mr. DeHorsey both left—I did not go with them after dinner to any place to canvass—I remained a short time, and then went to my own house—I returned to the Castle that evening a little before eight o'clock—I remember a Mr. Smith coming there very well indeed—something passed between me and him, which enables me to speak positively to the time—it must have been a little after eight o'clock—I should think I had been in the Castle twenty minutes before Mr. Smith came to me and had that conversation—I remained at the Castle the whole evening till after twelve o'clock—I did not see Mr. Miller at the hotel, after the time he quitted it at half past six o'clock—I do not believe Mr. Miller could have returned to the hotel, gone tip stairs, and have any conversation with persons there, without my knowing it, after the time I returned—it is possible—I went to hear Mr. Badnall speak, and was away a short time—that was at the Roebuck—that may be a hundred or a hundred and twenty yards from the Castle—I was gone a quarter of an hour or twenty minutes—that was after eight o'clock I am positive-with that exception, I was at the hotel all the evening.
JOHH MASON . I am the father of Mr. William Mason, and live at Newcastle. I remember the last election in July very well—I remember the nomination day on Saturday—the nomination took place about eight o'clock in the morning, or soon after-when the nomination and speeches were over, I went about canvassing with Mr. Miller and Mr. DeHorsey—I continued canvassing till dinner time, five o'clock—Mr. Miller and a number of people dined together at the Castle—after dinner, I and Mr. Miller left the Castle together-to the best of my recollection it was six o'clock or soon after—it was from six to half past six o'clock—we then went to a few houses, to voters who had been out when we went canvassing—I accompanied Mr. Miller during that after-dinner canvass—the canvass continued till eight o'clock, or soon after—Mr. Miller then went down to my son's—I walked with him down to there—I did not leave Mr. Miller after quitting the Castle after dinner, till I went with him to my son's—he did not return to the Castle at all in the meantime.
Q. As well as you can remember, what time should you say you arrived at your son's with Mr. Miller, that evening? A. Eight o'clock, or from that to half-past eight o'clock, at the outside—I did not look at any clock, but I believe it was at that time—it was quite light—I went into the lobby with Mr. Miller—I saw Mrs. Mason there—I did not stay any time there—I bid Mr. Miller good night—I left him there—I did not return to the Castle that night—I did not see Mr. Miller at all that night after leaving him at my son's house—after that I went up the London-road and through the market—I went by the Roebuck home—I went from my house to the Roebuck, and Mr. Badnall was addressing the crowd—I suppose it was not nine o'clock when he was speaking-l suppose it was nine o'clock or after when he finished-here was no speaking from the Castle this evening-down to the time of my leaving, there was no speaking from the Castle.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Will you swear it was not as late as ten o'clock when you were at the Roebuck. I should think it was not, for it was not dark—I did not notice the time—I think I can safely swear it was not ten o'clock—it was not ten o'clock to the best of my be-lief—I should think it was not—I go by the light—I swear positively it
was not ten o'clock—I know it by the time of the night—I did not look at the clock.
MR. KELLY. Q. Are you very well acquainted with the town of Newcastle? A. Yes-during the after-dinner canvass, between half-pan six o'clock and half-past eight o'clock, we went down Friar-street, along the Lower-street, to the bottom of Church-street, up Church-street, through the square, then into the market-place and High-street, and Penkel-street, across the London-road, down to my son's—I do not know the Royal Express—I do not remember going into any public-house in Lower-street, or any where—we might go into a beer-shop.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Will you swear you did not go into that beer-shop and canvass a voter there? A. I do not know the Royal Express.
CHARLES EMERY . I am a feltmaker, and live at Newcastle. I remember the last election—I remember Mr. Miller and his friends going down to the Castle—I went off to dine somewhere else—I Game back after I had finished my dinner, to the Castle—it was about six o'clock when I came back, I believe—I went along with the gentlemen on their canvas, Mr. Miller was one of them—I remember the names of the streets we went through—we went down Penkel-street and High-street, called in Paradise-street, came up Penkel-street again, down Friar-street, Lower-street, and up Church-street, or Church-lane some people call it—we went into Red Lion-square, and into the butchers' market—I remember a voter of the name of Joseph Massey—I left Mr. Miller canvassing Massey, and went away—I went into the Castle, and had one glass of ale—after that, I went into the street again, near the weighing machine, opposite the Castle—somebody pointed out Mr. Miller and another person to me—that was my old master—he has a nickname—they call him "Old Jersey"—that was intended for Mr. Mason—I followed them down Penkel-street, and saw them go into Mr. Mason's front garden—Mr. Miller and the old gentleman were the persons who went in—I mean old Mr. Mason—I am not positive, hut I think it was a little after eight o'clock—I then went to the Castle—there was a a great row there, and I did not stay—I went home—my wife was suprised that I came home so early.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. You are in the habit of being out late? A. No I am not, but it was election time—I am a voter—I voted for Mr. Miller and Mr. De Horsey—I had been canvassing that day—I was appointed by the committee to go with them canvassing—I served my time to a feltmaker—I cannot say exactly how long I have been out of my time—I have followed the hat-making since I was out of my time—I have a shop where I work—that is at Mr. Mason's now-if I chose to go and work I am his servant—I have worked for Mr. Halley and Mr. Eardley within the last twelve months—my hatting business is at Mr. Mason's, but I am in a way of business myself—I keep a house in Goose-street—I occupy the whole of it—there are two bed-rooms in it, and one sitting-room—I am a married man and have children—it is not a cottage, it is a warehouse—the warehouse is adjoining, it is where I do business—I have worked for two persons during the last twelve months—I do not recollect speaking to Mr. Mason about going canvassing at all—I did not follow" Old Jersey" and Mr. Miller to see what I could get for my vote—I had been with them before—I had no particular reason for following them down to Mr. Mason's house—I cannot recollect who pointed them out—I have thought of it, and cannot recollect—I was not up in Town at the sitting of the
committee—I did not know that I was to Borne to town till three hours before I came—that was last Tuesday—Mr. Hyatt was the person who asked me to come—it was not nearer to ten o'clock than him? when I saw Mr. Miller—the town-hall clock was close by—I looked at it, as I generally do when I go by—I have named to persons in my shop, that I saw Mr. Miller and Mr. Mason go home—I sell flour find corn in my shop.
Q. On your oath, have you earned 5 l. for yourself at your own business as a felt-maker, within the last twelve months, except at Mr. Mason's? A. I cannot say positively—I have a warehouse open to the street—I get my flour from one and another—I sometimes buy it of Mr. Mason—sometimes I bay six, sometimes ten, sometimes twelve sacks at a time—it cannot be three weeks ago since I had as many as six Backs—I suppose I am in Mr. Mason's debt—I always pay my way—I do not know that I have paid him all off—I probably owe him money, for they have had flour probably since I came away—I do not know but I may owe him money—I have no knowledge of how much I owe him-perhaps it may be 5 l.—it is not 50 l. or 40 l.—I keep two books in my business, in which I set down I what I lay out—I am now living it the Old Bell, in H—I believe Bagulay and Baddeley took me there—I think they both took me there.
MR. KELLY. Q. Is Mr. Hyatt a solicitor at Newcastle? A. Yes—he is now mayor of the town.
( Adjourned.)
Monday May 21st, 1838-Case of Gallimore continued.
MRS. MASON re-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did Mr. Murray come in while you were at tea with Mr. Miller that night, and stay until Mr. Miller went to bed? A. Yes.
MR. KELLY. Q. What time was that? A. A little after nine o'clock—I should suppose it was a little after nine o'clock by the light.
WILLIAM UPTON LEICESTER . On Saturday last, at noon, I received information that my attendance was required here—I beard so by the post, which must have left London on Friday—I was at the dinner-party at the Castle on Saturday, the nomination day—I left with several other persons, and proceeded on the canvass, after dinner—Mr. Miller was among them, as nearly as I can remember—he left the Castle that day about half-post six o'clock, after dinner—he parted from us somewhere about eight o'clock—I afterwards returned to the Castle—I reached it about half-past eight o'clock, and remained there till about eleven o'clock at night.
Q. From the time of Mr. Miller having left the Castle, as you think, about half-past six o'clock, after dinner, to canvass, did he at all return to the Cattle during that evening, or not? A. He did not—I was at the Castle with Mr. Mason that evening when Gallimore, Wall, and Plant came in, Mr. Mason was in Mr. De Horsey's sitting-room-when I saw them come I was inside Mr. De Horsey's sitting-room door, Mr. Mason was with me-when I saw these three persons, they came from the stain towards the room-door, where I was at the door—I believe they asked me to give them something-whether I did or not I am not quite certain, but I rather think I did—I mean that I gave them something to buy some ale—they did not ask, me any particular sum—they asked me to give them something to get them something to drink-whether I gave them any thing or not I cannot say, but I believe I did—I then went into
the room again—I was at the door of the room—I did not bear them say any more, either to me, to Mr. Mason, or anybody—I am not aware what they were doing—the room-door was shut, and they were in the passage—they asked for something to drink—they did not appear to be sober—they were tipsy—this was about nine o'clock in the evening, as near as possible I should suppose, or between that and ten o'clock—I should say nearer nine than otherwise—Mr. Miller was not there at that time—I had not seen him since I had left him at the canvass—I do not exactly remember which direction he went when I left him—there were no speeches made from the hotel that night.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. I believe you were one of Mr. Miller's committee? A. Yes, or Mr. De Horsey's—I have not been an active man in the elections at Newcastle—I have never been a canvasser for Mr. Miller before—I am not aware that one Joseph Bailey went with me—I will not swear he did not—I am not aware whether Sampson Bailey was with me—I will not swear that he was—I will not swear whether I gave any money to the men or not—that was not the first time I gave money to persons during an election-l am not in the habit of giving money at elections—I have done it perhaps a dozen or more times during the elections, in many days, 6 d. at a time—I will swear I have not given a great deal more than 6 d. a time at an election.
MR. KELLY. Q. What are you at Newcastle? A. A silk-manufacturer—I have a house in town—I have never given more than 1 s. at a time.
MR. CLARKSON addressed the Jury on behalf of the Defendant, and called the following Witnesses for the Defence.
SAMUEL PLANT . I am a shoemaker, living at Newcastle-under-lyne. I have resided there a great part of my life—I am a voter for that borough, as a freeman—I know Mr. Miller—I voted for him and Mr. De Horsey at the last election—I also know Mr. William Mason—I remember meeting him on the first day of the canvassing—I remember going to the castle on the evening of the nomination day, between nine and ten o'clock-Gallimore and Wall were with me—we went up stairs to the first floor, and staid there a short time—I saw Mr. William Mason and Mr. Henry Miller there—I saw them go into a small room—I got dose to the room-door, and I heard something said by the persons inside the room—Mr. Miller made answer to Mr. Mason, and said he could not think the reason why he was so ill treated on his canvass: that he could not go into a poor man's house but what he was upbraided of things that were left unpaid at the former elections.
Q. You have said that Mr. Miller said that in answer to Mr. Mason; had Mr. Mason said any thing before that to Mr. Miller? A. Mr. Mason stated that there was 900 l. had been expended on electioneering purposes; Mr. Miller said he could not think how it was—Mr. Miller asked him what did be think of it, and Mr. Mason said, "I shall want 700 l. to buy seven-score of burgesses, or else you will lose your election"—Mr. Miller made answer that he would not lose his election for the sake of money, he should have it—Mr. Miller said he wished he had never coalesced with Mr. De Horsey, and the election would not have cost him so much money—then we made ourselves known that we were at the door-Wall went into the room first, and I followed, and Gallimore stood about half a foot in the room behind us—he got into the room about half a foot—we all three went in—Mr. Mason and Mr. Miller seemed rather confused to find that we were there, and had heard the conversation—Mr. Mason immediately put us out of the room before
him, and offered to give us half a crown to drink his health, which we refused, and Mr. Miller threw himself down on the bed—it was a small room, Just to hold one bed, and as much room as a person could go round—there was a light in the room—it appeared to be a candle—after that we came away, and left the Castle—I believe, there were speeches made from the castle in the course of that evening, but very snort ones—they were made from the front bow-window—I believe Mr. Miller end Mr. De Horsey both spoke some few words.
Cross-examined by MR. KELLY. Q. Where had you been, immediately., Before you went to the Castle? A. I had been from nine o'clock in the morning till eight o'clock at night about three quarters of a mile out of Newcastle at work—I did not come straight from the place at which I had been at work, to the Cattle—I went home and got my tea, and washed and shaved myself—I live outside the town—I went direct from home to the top of Church-street, and staid there; a few minutes in the street, and heard the chimes play nine o'clock—I next went down to where the crowd was drawing, towards the Castle Hotel—I went amongst the rest of the people that were there—I met with Gallimore and Wall in the crowd—we then went into the Castle hotel together—it might be near half-past nine o, clock when I got there—I believe it to have been half past nine o'clock—I will not speak to a minute.
Q. It was dark then so as to require a light in the bed-room you speak of? A. It was—it was a back room, and that was not so light as the front room, and there was a candle burning in the room where I saw Miller and Mason,-when you get on the fist floor, you turn on the left hand, and there is a small door that leads to the bed-room, on the top of the first landing, up the first flight of stairs—it was on the first floor, not the second, the first flight of stairs—I saw Mr. Miller and Mason go into the room, nobody else-and when I afterwards went into the room, there was nobody else there—it was a small room—I could have seen if there had been anybody else there.
Q. And immediately after this conversation took place, and you had been offered money you would not take, you went down and went away? A. Yes; I am sure of that—I never communicated with any one there—I do not know "what the rest did—they went away with me—there were persons passing and recasting, they might have spoken or not, I did not hear them—we got the door open a foot, as near as I can recollect, while they were talking—there were several people passing up and down stairs, to go to different rooms, there ware, no persons passing through the lobby towards the room door, only passing the stairs to go to other rooms—I saw Mr. Miller and Mr. Mason go into this room, and through an observation that Wall made,, we followed them—they got into the room first—I was about four yards from them—I stood with my back to them—they passed me and went into this room—I was standing in the lobby, close to the entrance of the lobby from the stairs-Gallimore and Wall were alto in the lobby—they passed all three of us—my back was towards them as they passed—I cannot tell whether the backs of the others were towards them—I could not, tell whether they came up stairs or out of a room, but they passed me, and went into the bed-room-neither of them spoke to me—I did not hear them speak to any of the others—I could not see whether they came up stairs or out of a room—I did not see them till they had pasted me in the lobby.—I did not see them come up stairs.
Q. Upon your oath, before the Committee of the House of Commons,
did you not swear that you saw them come up stairs into the room? I did not—I swore that I stood with my back towards them—I did swear that they came up stairs—I was examined by counsel there-stated that I. heard them come, I could not tell whether they came stairs, or out of a room, as I stood with my back to them—I did not say they came up directly to the room.
Q. Upon your giving an answer like that I have mentioned to you did not the counsel say, "Up stairs into the room," and did not yon say "Yes?" A. No—I had been standing a short time in the lobby before they came—we had not stood there long—it might be ten minutes, more less—I am quite sure I did not swear that they came up stairs into the lobby-and I do not know where they came from, any more than seeing them pass me—I never said that I heard Mr. Miller and Mr. De Horsey make speeches that evening—I did not hear them myself, but I believe there were some—I heard no speeches that evening—the first person I heard speak in the bed-room was Mr. Miller—he said he could not think how it he was so ill treated on his canvass—he could not go to a poor man's house to solicit him for his vote, but what he was upbraided for things left unpaid on his former canvass—that was the sum and substance of the matter—I believe that was the first thing I heard—I will swear it was-when Mr. Miller had said that, Mr. Mason said he had expended 900 l. that Mr. Miller had already advanced on electioneering purposes, that was the observation of Mr. Mason in return—Mr. Miller then asked Mr. Mason what he thought of it—Mr. Mason said he should want 700 l. to buy seven-score of burgesses, or else he would lose his election-upon that, Mr. Miller said he would not lose his election for the sake of that sum of money, he should have it—and then he said he was Sorry he entered into the coalition, he said he wished he had not entered into the coalition with De Horsey, or the election would not have cost him so much money—that was the substance of the conversation, and the order in which each of them spoke—I am sure of that—I swore before the Committee of the House of Commons, that Mr. Miller asked Mr. Mason what he had done with the 900 l. already advanced in that election—I swore that, and it is true-what I have now stated is true—Mr. Mason said he had expended 900 l. in election purposes—Mr. Miller asked him what he had done with the 900 l., and then Mr. Mason said, in reply, that he had expended every farthing in electioneering purposes.
Q. Why did you not tell me that at first? A. I have stated it now—I told you as far as my recollection brought me to the thought of it—MR. Mason said he must have 700 l. to buy seven-score of burgesses—he did not say, "He must buy about seven hundred men, which would cost him about 700 l. "—I did not swear that before the Committee of the House of Commons, not to the best of my knowledge-no, I did not—on being asked by the Committee, "What question did he ask Mason before he said this? I did not say he asked him what he thought of it, and he said he thought he must buy about seven hundred men, and that would cost about seven-saw pounds—I did not say that he said he must buy about seven hundred men—I saw Mr. Mason and Mr. Miller go into the room together—I had voted for Mr. Miller four times, and knew him perfectly well—I am perfectly sure—I saw them go into the room together—I know Mr. Leicester—I cannot say that I saw him and Mr. Mason both together at the Castle, but I saw them distinctly that night both of them, in the Castle—I saw Mr. Leicester going up stairs as I was up in the lobby—I do not know where he went to—I cannot say
that he was alone—there were persons passing and re-passing—I cannot say where he went—I do not recollect seeing him and Mr. Mason together—I saw them both that night—I will swear I did not ask one of them for money, nor did I hear either of the others—we waited about ten minutes in the lobby—I did not wait for any particular thing any more than the rest of the people that were about—I had no particular reason-at electioneering times they are in and out, and no notice is taken of them—I had no reason for waiting there—I went to look about as the rest of the people did—I had no reason, for one thing or another—I had been working from nine o'clock in the morning till eight o'clock at night—I did not go to listen for any thing I might catch up-when I saw Mr. Mason and Mr. Miller go in—I went in consequence of Wall observing that he thought there was something up there, and then we went to listen—that was when they went into the bed-room—I had no particular reason for standing them ten minutes myself—I talked with Gallimore and Wall, and we continued talking till Miller and Mason passed, and then we all three went close to he door—they had not been in half a minute before we were at the door—I pushed the door open a little further myself with my hand.
Q. Who do you work for? A. A man of the name of Robert' Rhodes cannot tell exactly how long we were altogether at the Cattle, not any time—I did not notice the time-when I left the Castle I went to the Globe—I cannot say whether that was one of Mr. Badnall's houses—I do not know whether it was Mr. Miller's, or De Horsey-s, or Badnall's—there were different people in the house—I do not know whether House is the master of the Globe, or Mr. Hope—I do not know whether the house is in the in-terest of Mr. Badnall or not—I found some persons there—I cannot say whether they were the friends of Mr. Badnall or Mr. Miller—they might be fiends of both—I cannot say whether they were friends of one or the other—I do not know.
Q. You. went down after this; it is not true that the constable was ceiled, and you were put out? A. No; I had not had any ale that day—I was perfectly sober, and Gallimore the same, and Wall—I did not sec any reason to say they were intoxicated.
MR. JONES. Q. Were you in work up to the time you were taken into custody? A. Yes; and I left about fourteen shillings' worth of work when I was taken—I have been, in Newgate ever since I was first apprehended, which was six weeks last Friday night—I was in bed, and a person came and called me up—I do not know that he was a constable—he is assistantoverseer of the poor-house—I went from the Castle to the Globe—I do not know whose house that was—I stated at the Globe the conversation I had heard between Mr. Miller and Mr. Mason at the Castle—I saw Mr. John Brostock and Mr. Thursfield there—there was a xlarge room full of persons.
MR. KELLY. Q. Was what you said there taken down in writing? A. I did not see it myself.
COURT. Q. You knew those two persons to whom you spoke? A. Yes; I do not know whether they voted for Mr. Miller or Mr. Badnall.
ENOCH WALL . I am a tailor living at Newcastle—I have lived there all my life within about six or eight months—I am a freeman there—I know Mr. Miller—I remember the last election—I voted for Mr. Miller and Mr. Badnall—I know the Castle—I remember the nomination-day before the election—I met with Gallimore and Plant that night—I went with them to the Castle—we went up the first flight of stairs-when we got there I saw Mr. Mason come up the stain, and be went into a small bed-room—there
was somebody gone in before, but I did not see them—the room into which I saw Mr. Mason go into was on the left hand of the lobby-when I saw him go in I said to Gallimore and Platt, "By God there is something up let us go and see"—on that we went—we were as close to the door as we could be—the door was a little open—I heard some convesation in the room—I did not particularly know the voices of the people who were conversing—I afterwards discovered that the persons in the room were Mr. Mason and Mr. Miller—I heard one say to the other that he wanted 700 l. to buy seven-score burgesses with, and he asked where the 900 l. was that had been already advanced—I cannot say which it was said that—he said it was all gone for electioneering purposes, and he said he would not lose the election for the sake of money.
Q. Was it the same person said all this? A. There were different void—I cannot tell which said which—there was more said, but I did not discern it myself, I could not comprehend what it was-upon that I went into the room, and saw Mr. Miller and Mr. Mason in there, and Mr. Miller threw himself backwards on the bed—Mr. Mason rushed at us, and pushed us out of the room, and offered us half a crown, but we would not have it—we were in the lobby—we came out of the room—Mr. Mason came out with us, pushing us before him, and offered in half a crown, but we would not have it—I knew Mr. Miller and Mr. Mason before that day—I was sober that night, I never touched any drink all the day—I went from the Castle to the Globe-Gallimore and Plant went with me—there were a good many persons there in the room—Mr. Brostock was one—we mentioned at the Globe the conversation we heard at the Castle—I have been in custody on this charge six weeks last Friday on this charge.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. you expect to be tried on it, don't you? A. I am on trial now—I mean to tell the jury that I think 11 am on trial now—I am as a witness for one of the party—I do not know the rule of the Court—I saw a man tried once—I never knew there were assizes at Newcastle—I saw Mr. Mason go up the stairs—he had to pass us in the lobby before he got to the door—I cannot tell how far I was from the door at that time, three, or four yards—I saw nobody else with Mr. Mason—there was somebody else went in before him—I saw him go in by himself—there was a light in the lobby so that I could see anybody that passed—I believe some one passed me shortly before Mr. Mason-yes, there did—I did not see that person, my head was turned the other way—that was not half a minute before I saw Mr. Mason—somewhere about that time—I heard Mr. Mason come up the stairs, when the other passed—they did not come up together—they left the door open—they spoke up, not very loud—I could hear distinctly—the first thing I heard was something about 900 l., I believe—he asked where the 900 l. was that had been already advanced-to the best of my knowledge that was the first thing I heard—after that he said he wanted 700 l. to buy seven-score burgesses with—I am sure of that.
Q. Did you ever swear it was 500 l. he said he wanted? A. I believe I did, but it was a mistake—I did not swear to it a second time, that I know of—I will not swear that I did not—I was close to the door-Plant and Gallimore went along with me close to the door.
Q. Why did you tell the Committee of the House of Commons that it was 500 l. that you heard mentioned to buy the seven scores? A. it was a mistake—I did not tell the Committee it was a mistake before I left them—I believe that was not said before the 900 l. was mentioned—I will
swear it was not—there is no mistake about that—I did not swear before the Committee that the 500 l. was mentioned, and the 900 l. after—I will not swear that I did not—I will not swear that I did not tell them that the 500 l. was mentioned before the 900 l.—I believe the 900 l. was mentioned first—I will swear it—I believe I swore that the 500 l. was mentioned first, I in answer to that it was asked what was done with the 900 l.—the 900 l. was mentioned first—I suppose it must have been a mistake when I swore before the Committee that the 500 l. was mentioned first—I believe I told the Committee that the first words I heard said were "He wanted 500 l. to buy seven-score burgesses"—I might say that the first words I heard were the 500 l.—I did not write down what I said—I do not know whether it was taken down—I believe I said that the first words I heard said were that he wanted 500 l. to buy seven-score burgesses—(that was true,) but lit was 700 l.—the words were 700 l.—the best person in the world may make a mistake sometimes.
Q. Have you been talking to Plant? A. Not about the discourse—I have been talking to him in prison about this trial, but not about that—I went to the lobby with Plant and Gallimore—we were in the street before that—I cannot say how long I had been with Plant before I went to the lobby—about five minutes—I met Plant in the street—Gallimore and I were together, and we went to the Castle hotel—I might have been five or ten minutes in the lobby before I heard this conversation—I cannot say.
Q. Mr. Miller did not pass you? A. Some one pasted—I was not looking at him at the time he must pass—I swore I did not see him pass me at all—I did not say "I did not see Mr. Miller pass"—Mr. Mason was no great way from me when he passed—he might be half a yard or more from me—I saw him distinctly, and there was nothing to prevent his teeing me—I was two or three yards from the door that they left open—I did not hear every word.
Q. Did not he speak up like a man? A. Yes—I did not see any constable or any person pretending to be a constable there.
Q. Did you hear the speeches? A. I heard a word or two from Mr. De Horsey—that was between nine and ten o'clock—I cannot tell what it was—I was opposite the machine-house—he spoke from the bow window of the stairs of the Castle—that was before we heard the conversation swear that—Mr. Gallimore was with me at the time—I am sure of that—I was not talking to Gallimore—I never spoke to him then—I stood aside of him—he was close beside me, coming as if he came from the Castle, mind he stood by my side—I do not know whether he was looking up at the window—he was by side of me, but when Mr. De Horsey was speaking, he was coming from the Castle—he stood beside me, but not at the time Mr. De Horsey was speaking.
Q. Did you not tell me he had been with you a few minutes—is that true or false? A. False—I had not been in any public-house with Gallimore that night—I had been at work all day—I left work at half-past seven o'clock—I was playing with my children from then till between nine and ten o'clock—I had been in a public-house in the of the day, and drank—we were not turned out of the Castle that night—the head ostler was at the bottom of the stairs—there was nobody at the top—I know Baddeley—he was not at the top of the stairs—I did not say any thing to the head ostler—I do not know whether Gallimore did—he permitted us to go up——it was between nine and ten o'clock—I heard the conversation, I mean to
I believe he was stationed to keep people from going up—I saw some persons at the Globe.
Q. Who were the persons you saw at the Globe? A. They were some of Mr. Badnall's friends—I went before the Committee of the House of Commons with Mr. B—in a cab —I believe he had not given me any thing to drink—I will not swear he had not—I never saw him before that day—I met him in the road as I was coming down to the House of commons—he was in a cab —he stopped, and took me into it—I had never spoken to him before on that day, nor he to me.
Q. Had you ever spoken to him? A. Yes, many times, but not on that day—Ashley was with me, and a person named Renham—they were witnesses—he did not take them.
Q. About how wide did they leave this door open? A. Two or three inches—there was a handle to the door—there was only us three together—when we rushed in Mr. Miller threw himself on the bed, and Mason rushed out and offered us half a crown—they were very much surprised at us—I think they did not say anything—I will swear they did not—Mr. Miller did not say anything to me.
JOHN HAWKINS . I live at Newcastle-under-Lyne, and am an engraver, in a branch of the pottery business. I know Gallimore and Wall—I cannot say that I know Plant very well—I remember the nomination day at the last election—in the course of that evening I was at the Globe public-house—I remember seeing Gallimore there, I should think it was half-past ten o'clock, or thereabouts—Gallimore mentioned something to me as having occurred at the Castle that evening.
JOHN BROSTOCK . I am a grocer, at Newcastle-under-Lyne, and have carried on business there twenty years. I know the prisoner Gallimore—I remember the nomination day of the last election—I remember being at the Globe public-house that evening—I saw Gallimore there—it was after ten o'clock in the evening, I cannot say exactly—I heard him make a communication there of something that had taken place at the Castle that evening.
Cross-examined by MR. KELLY. Q. What time of the night was it when he arrived there? A. It was after ten o'clock—I cannot speak positively—there were a great many persons there—I cannot say exactly how many—eight or ten perhaps—it is a house in the interest of Mr. Badnall—I was not examined before the Committee.
LYDIA HAYES . I am the wife of Samuel Hayes—he is a hair-dresser, at Newcastle-under-Lyne. I remember Saturday, the day of the nomination for the borough at the last election—I was at the front of the Castle hotel between the hours of nine and ten o'clock that night—I went on purpose to hear Mr. Miller—I saw him at the window—he addressed the people outside—it was a few words, to the best of my recollection—I know the Roebuck public-house—I had heard Mr. Badnall that night before I heard Mr. Miller.
Cross-examined by MR. KELLT. Q. Were you examined before the Committee of the House of Commons? A. No—I do not live very far from the Castle—I was at my mother's house this night, that is nearer to the Castle than where I live—it is in another street—I was standing in the street when I heard the speech—I had come from the iron-market—I went on purpose to hear Mr. Miller—I had not heard him speak the night before, nor the night before that—I had heard Mr. Badnall speak the night before—I had not heard Mr. Miller speak at that election at all before—Mr. Miller
was just putting his head out, as I was standing there—I was not there two minutes—I heard no one else make a speech—there was no particular noise or confusion that I heard of—there were a great many people in the street—they were quiet while Mr. Miller spoke—he said he was fatigued, and requested them to be early at the poll on Monday morning.
Q. Where were you between nine and ten o'clock on Friday? A. At my own house—I cannot answer where I was on Thursday—I do not now where I was on Wednesday, but at home, I dare say—my husband not in London—I knew nothing of this till the night before I started—I started on Thursday—no one applied to me to come up here—my aunt, and my husband, and I were talking, and I said I remembered the circumtance—Mr. Harding's clerk gave me a notice last Thursday night to come—I was wrong when I said I left on Thursday—I left Newcastle on Friday morning, and arrived here on Friday evening—I came by the railway—I remained at Newcastle till after the election—I was not present at the nomination—I went to hear Mr. Badnall, at the Roebuck, because I wished to hear him—I cannot say how long I stopped listening to him, a considerable time—I had not heard Mr. De Horsey make a speech, during the election—I know Mr. Badnall—my husband is a voter—he voted for Mr. Badnall and Mr. De Horsey—the first time I spoke to Mr. Badnall was last Saturday at the inn, across the way—I do not know the name of is.
FREDERICK THOMAS APPLEBT . I am an apprentice to Mr. Josiah Roby, a tea-dealer, at Newcastle-under-Lyne; his shop is right opposite the Castle hotel. I remember the evening of the nomination of the last election—I saw Mr. Miller that evening, at the Castle hotel, between the hours of nine and ten o'clock—he was speaking to the crowd from the window of the Castle hotel—I heard him speaking—he said he had a successful canvass, and he hoped they would be early at the poll on Monday morning.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did you heat all he said? A. Yes—I cannot remember what else he said—he did not speak any thing like half an hour—about a couple of minutes—he had not his hat on—he put his head out of the window, and spoke about two minutes—I was about three yards from the house—I cannot recollect any thing else he said—I believe he did say something else, but I cannot remember what—I went for the purpose of hearing the speeches—I went across when they were cheering him—I did not wait to hear if any body else spoke—there were other people about there at the window—I did not know the persons of the gentlemen who were at the window with him—I cannot remember that I had seen them before—no one spoke beside Mr. Miller, as I heard—I was alone—I did not take notice of the people—I came to London on Friday night—I was first asked to come on Thursday—several gentlemen desired me to come up—Mr. was one—I was not before the Committee of the House of Commons.
Q. Have you seen Mr. Badnall since you were in town? A. Yes, passing up the street—I stopped and spoke to him—I did not walk with him—this was on Saturday—I have been in no house with him—Mr. Beardmore came to me at Newcastle—I have no vote—my master has—he gave it to Mr. Badnall.
— HEDGE. I am a tailor, living on the Green in Newcastle-under-Lyne. I remember the Saturday, the day of the nomination before
the last election—I worked till about eight o'clock in the evening of the day—I washed, and changed my clothes afterwards, and then left home for the purpose of hearing the speeches I expected to hear from the candi-dates—I passed through the Lower-street—I know Mr. Miller and Mr. William Mason—I saw them together in the Lower-street about a quarter past eight o'clock, to the best of my knowledge—they went up Church lane—I went in another direction—not more than twenty minutes after that I saw them in High-street—they were then going to Mr. Mason's house, or towards the Castle hotel—it is all in one direction—there was a crowd in front of the Roebuck—that is in the High-street—I saw go them through the crowd as far as I could distinguish them—I heard Mr. Badnall address the electors from the Roebuck—I then went to the castle hotel—there were a large number of persons in front—that was a very few minutes after I left the Roebuck—I walked straight to the Castle—it was between nine and ten o'clock—I know the person of Mr. Miller—I know his voice—I did not see either Mr. Miller or Mr. De Horsey while it front of the hotel, not to the best of my knowledge—after I had been short time in front, I heard the cry, "The canvassers are com ing"—I heard a very short speech from the window—I cannot tell who that was from—I was not able at that time to distinguish the persons of the people at the window—it was rather dusk—I believe it was Mr. Miller's voice, but I cannot tell—I have frequently heard Mr. Miller speak.
Cross-examined by MR. KELLY. Q. Are you a voter? A. Yes, and voted for Mr. Badnall.
JURY. Q. Are you quite certain you saw Mr. Miller as late as nine o'clock in the street? A. After it wanted about twenty-five minutes to nine o'clock.
COURT. Q. How late will you fix that you saw him? A. I cannot tell how late it was—it was before Mr. Badnall made his speech—after I saw Mr. Miller and Mr. Mason in Lower-street, I then went and saw some other persons—I saw them again about a quarter to nine o'clock.
MR. KELLY. Q. To the best of your belief, what hour was it you saw Mason and Miller together in Lower-street? A. About a quarter past eight o'clock—I gave over work about eight o'clock, I washed, and changed my clothes, and then went straight to the Lower-street—I remember looking at my watch as soon as I gave over work, it was then eight o'clock—my watch is generally right, and from that I think I saw Mr. Miller at a quarter-past eight o'clock, and then again about twenty minutes after—that was the last time that I can swear positively that I saw him that night.
JURY. Q. What is the distance from the Castle to Lower-street? A. Not many minutes' walk; perhaps two or three.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. How long after you saw Mr. Miller in the High-street was it that you heard somebody make a speech from the Castle hotel? A. I cannot say how long—I should think an hour.
GERRARD JACKSON . I live at Newcastle. I remember the Saturday, the nomination day, before the last election—my house is on the Green—I left home that night about eight o'clock—I looked at the clock before I started—I went to be shaved, and after that I went with my wife opposite the Roebuck, and stood there a while to hear Mr. Badnall make his speech—after that I went down opposite the Castle hotel, to hear Mr. Miller make his speech—I got to the Castle about half-past nine o'clock, to the best of my judgment—I am certain it was past nine o'clock—there were a great
many persons there—I went to the front of the door—I heard tome one cry out" They are coming," or "The canvassers are coming"—on looking towards the door, I saw Mr. Miller and Mr. Mason on the steps of the castle hotel—there were other persons with them—they went into the castle hotel—after that I saw Mr. Miller at the window—he made a short speech—he addressed the electors on the good success he had met with at this canvass, and wished them to be early at the poll on Monday morning—it was getting near ten o'clock then—it was nearer ten o'clock than nine—I swear that positively.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. What kind of night was it? A. It was getting towards dusk at night—I could see quite clearly—there was a great lamp over the doorway, so that nobody could have been off seeing Mr. Miller at the window—a man three or four yards off must have seen him quite clearly—he merely addressed the electors on the good success he had met with on his canvass, and wished them to be early at the poll on Monday morning, and bid them good night.
Q. Have you been comparing notes with Mr. Appleby? A. No, I have not—the window projects out—there is some kind of place over the door—there were some Newcastle men in the window with him—there was Mr. Mason, and others beside, and Mr. Bailey was there—he is a clock-maker—and others—I cannot tell the names of any others—I do not know whether Mr. Bailey is here—I came to town some few days ago—I think it was the last Sunday but one—I saw Mr. Badnall on Saturday, and I see him now—I have only seen him twice—I have not seen him more than once before to-day—I spoke to him on that Saturday, but I have not to-day—I only spoke to him on that Saturday—I saw him at the public—house over the way—he was there with me, and several other persons—there was Mr. and Mrs. Hedges, John Broomhall, and a young man of the name of Bartlett—I cannot say whether there was any more that I knew—I was three or four minutes with him.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did you know that the witnesses had been turned out of Court? A. No, I did not know any thing about it—it was Mr. Bailey, the clock-maker, that was at the window.
CHARLES BARTLETT . I was employed at the last election as flag—carrier, for Mr. Miller.
