I remember the day of the nomination—I was in front of the Castle hotel—there was a crowd there—I was waiting to hear Mr. Miller's speech—it might be between nine and ten o'clock—I heard Mr. Miller address the crowd from the window of the Castle hotel—I went into the house after that—I know the large room from the window of which the candidates speak—I went up the stairs leading to that room—there were several persons there—I saw Mr. Miller and Mr. Mason there—they were in conversation together—I cannot say whether they saw me—they were coming towards the door of the room from which they speak—I opened the door of the room and saw them, and they were coming toward the door, as if they were coming out—when I saw them, I closed the door and went down two steps—they came out, and went across towards the committee-room—there is a bed-room there, a small room—I did not see them go into any room, but they went towards the committee-room—that would lead also towards the bed-room—whilst I was there, there were persons passing up and down stairs—I left the Castle about ten o'clock, or it might be afterwards—I am sure it was as much as half-past nine o'clock.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Have you had any quarrel with Mr. Mason? A. No, I have been in his employ, but am not now—I
cannot say exactly how long I have left—this Saturday night was not a light, it was a dark night—I heard Mr. Miller speak—I cannot exactly say what he said—he did not say he was insulted by the poor voters—he said he was much fatigued, and he hoped they would be early on Monday. morning in coming forward—his hat was off—I saw him clearly—I know Mr. Badnall—I saw him at Newcastle, and I have seen him since I come to town, going along the Old Bailey, and at the New inn, I think they call it, just across the way—I did not go there to see him—I did not go on purpose to see him—I could not be long with him, perhaps it might be five minutes—that was not to-day—it was on Saturday—I cannot say that I was ever in the employ of a man of the name of Cox—I do not recollect the name—I worked at Mr. Cox's shop some years since—I do not know Union-hall—I was taken to some police-office, and went to gaol for a month.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. What sort of a shop was it? A. My work was there, and I was going to take it to another place, and somebody said that I was going to take it away—it was not Mr. Cox's work—I was never tried before that time, and then I was sent by the Justice for a month.
COURT. Q. Were you tried in any Court, or committed by the Magistrate? A. I do not know—I was before some persons—I was tried at a bar something similar to this—I cannot say what place they called it—I had never been in a Court of Justice before that time—I believe there was a Jury—it was several years ago—I cannot say exactly how long, it may be six, seven, or eight years.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Have you been working for Mr. Mason since that time? A. Yes.
CHARLES FRANCIS . I am a trunkmaker, living in Lower-street, Newcastle. I know the Royal Express beer-shop, or public-house—I was there on Saturday, the nomination evening before the last election, between eight and nine o'clock—I know Mr. Miller, the member—he came into the Royal Express beer-shop—I do not know Mr. Mason—there were several gentlemen with him—the name of the landlord is Mr. Boot—some gentleman with Mr. Miller asked him for his vote in his hearing—I was coming out of the house at the same time—I afterwards saw Mr. Miller canvassing the voters in Lower-street, and I saw him at the Castle hotel—before that I saw him at the bottom of Church-lane—I was at the Castle about half-past nine o'clock, or a quarter to ten—I went there to hear Mr. Miller address the people—while I was standing there, I saw Mr. Miller—he went into the Castle hotel, and a gentleman had hold of his arm—when he got in he came to the window, and made a short speech—I heard that.
Cross-examined by MR. KELLY. Q. What time was this? A. Nearer ten o'clock than nine—a young man of the name of Thomas Patmore was with me, he is a shoemaker—I have lived some years in Newcastle—I am not a voter—I am no relation or connexion of Gallimore's—my wife is no relation of him, nor his wife—we did not marry two sisters—there is no connexion, or kindred, between my wife and his—my wife's maiden name was Amelia Downs—I do not know who the prisoner married—his brother married a sister of my wife's.
Q. What is the reason you did not tell me that? A. That is no connexion of mine—I never thought about it—it was getting dusk, but quite light enough for anybody to distinguish a man's face—I swear that.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Would that depend upon how near he was to the
window? A. I was exactly under the window—I know that Patmore has left Newcastle since that time.
GUILTY. Aged 43.— Judgment Respited.
ESSEX CASES.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18380514-1365]
1365. THOMAS SULLINS was indicted for killing and slaying Thomas Boulter.
DANIEL BOULTER . I am a waterman, and live in Ann's-row, Bromley; the deceased, Thomas Boulter, was my brother. On Monday morning, the 26th of March, I heard what was about to take place, and went down to the marshes in Essex, near the river Lea—I saw Curtis, Tantony, and Blaker, and my brother come in a boat—I saw the prisoner there—a fight took place between my brother and Blaker—the prisoner picked my brother up, and put him on another man's knee—he continued to do so during the fight, and he wished him to leave off—they fought about thirty-five minutes—I saw Blaker strike my brother a blow with the right hand under the left ear—it appeared a chance blow—he fell down, and never was sensible afterwards—he was put into a boat and taken away.
MARGARET WILSON . I live in Gardiner-place, Poplar. I knew the deceased—I was present at the Angel public-house, when there was a dispute between him and Blaker—I was present on Monday, the 26th of March, at the marshes, hearing a fight was to take place—I did not see him pick him up—I was not very near—the prisoner came there in the boat with the rest, and remained till it was over—I did not see Boulter fall the last time.
WILLIAM BAIN . I am a surgeon, and live in Brunswick-street, Poplar. On Monday, the 26th of March, I was called in to see the deceased—I found him at hi, lodging, in Leicester-street—he was insensible, and never became sensible—he died about two hours after I arrived—there were several contusions on his face and head—there was a post mortem examination—the immediate cause of death was the rupture of a Wood-vessel in the head, which might be produced by a violent blow in a fight—he had every appearance of having been engaged in fighting—there was a blow immediately below the left ear—I have not a doubt death was occasioned by blows received in fighting—after having heard the evidence, I believe that was the occasion of his death.
Prisoner's Defence. On the 26th of March Boulter called me up, as I live with his mother—I went down to see the fight, but not to be second—I wiped his face several times—I tried to persuade him to leave off fighting—he said no, he would not; he had brought forty people there to see the fight, and he would not make fools of them—at last I caught hold of him, and said he should leave off fighting, I would have no more of it—Blaker came up to shake hands with him, but he got out of my hands, and fell down on the grass—I took him to his mothers, and went for the surgeon.
GUILTY. Aged 29.— Recommended to mercy — Confined One Month .
Before Mr. Justice Patteson.
[trialAccount t18380514-1366]
1366. WILLIAM STEVENS was indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Samuel Wood, about the hour of twelve, in the night of the 30th of September, 1836, with intent to steal, and stealing therein part of a snuff-box, value 1 d.; and 13 buttons, value 4 d. his property.
SAMUEL WOOD . I live in Barking-side, Essex, and am a labouring man. In September, 1836, I occupied a cottage there—nobody lived in but I and my wife—on the morning of the 30th of September I and my wife went out to work about five or six o'clock—the door was fastened with a chain—it was not locked—the bed-room window was shut, I am certain—it is on the ground-floor, and is a sash-window—I fastened the door with a chain, and tied it with a string—we returned about twelve o'clock at night, and found the door fastened, as we had left it—the window was taken out, and stood down, inside the room—I opened the door and went in, and found the prisoner partly under the bed—the things were taken out of the boxes and drawers, and lying all about the room—I knew the prisoner—he pulled the top of my snuff-box out of his pocket, and gave it to me—I had left it in the box in the morning—the moon shone in at the window, and my wife fetched a light—I am sure it was him—I asked him what business he had there—he said he thought he was in his own house—I cannot say he was sober, nor yet altogether tipsy—I let him go, being a neighbour—I never saw him again till he was apprehended—he lived close to me, but I never saw him—I did not trouble myself to inquire for him—I had not heard anybody moving about before I went in, but we perceived a light in the house.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. It was a back window; I believe it was open? A. Yes—we came up to prosecute the prisoner, after he was taken on another charge—Smith, the officer, came after me—the prisoner had not been living in the county all the time, that I know of—this is the top of the snuff-box—( producing it)—it is of no value—his moths gave my wife thirteen buttons in the morning—it was not dark when we went out in the morning—we were away the whole day—I did not go before the Magistrate to make a charge—the Magistrate sent for me—the constable went into his house, and I after him, that night, after I let him go, but did not find him.
COURT. Q. What things were taken out of the boxes, and scattered about the room? A. Clothes and bed-furniture.
HARRIETT WOOD . I am the wife of the prosecutor. I went out, and fastened the door myself, and the windows—the bed-room window was shut—I got some buttons from somebody the next morning.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18380514-1367]
1367. WILLIAM STEVENS was again indicted for stealing, on the 5th of April, 1 watch value 26 s.; 1 watch-chain, value 2 d.; 1 watch-key, value 2 d.; and 1 fourpenny-piece; the goods and money of Daniel Murray, from his person.
DANIEL MURRAY . On the 5th of April I was at Hunt's beer-shop, at Barking—I had a watch in my watch-pocket—I had been drinking—I went out into the back-part of the premises, and fell asleep—I know my watch was safe when I went out of the door—Willis, my mate, missed it before I did.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSOH. Q. You had been drinking rather much perhaps? A. I do not think I had, but J had plenty—I cannot whether I had my watch in my pocket when I was in the privy—in buttoning, to put my watch into my fob, I may have missed my fob, and dropped it.
THOMAS SMITH . I am a constable. In consequence of information I apprehended the prisoner, on the 10th of May, about ten o'clock at night, Hollingsworth's beer-shop, which is rather above a mile from Hunt's—I found the watch on him—before I found it he said he would show me the an he got the watch from—we let him go.
Cross-examined. Q. Were you constable of the parish in 1836? A. yes—the prisoner had been drinking, but was not tipsy—he could walk—e had not been drinking to excess—he was perfectly sober and sensible—knew where to find the prisoner respecting the last case—I took him before the Magistrate after I had this charge—Wood lives at Barking-side, four miles from Barking.
JAMES HUNT . I was fourteen years old last November—I am the son John Hunt who keeps the beer-shop at Barking—I know the prisoner—he was at my father's house on the 5th of April—I was in the next privy Murray between six and seven o'clock—the prisoner came to me and lid, "Where is Dan?"—I said, "In the next privy asleep"—he laid, "Let him have an hour or two's sleep, it will do him good"—and then he went away. I saw no more of him.
JAMES WILLIS . I was with Murray at Hunt's beer-shop on the 5th of April—I recollect his going out of the house—I remained in the house—I was sober—he went out of the house about ten minutes after six o'clock at night—I went to look for him about a quarter past six o'clock, and found him asleep in the privy—I cannot tell whether he had his watch with him or not—I shut the door and left him there—I went again in about half an hour, he was still asleep—I examined, and his watch was not in his fob, nor about his person—when I could not find it I awoke him—I took him back into the house—the prisoner was then in the house—I said in his hearing that Murray had lost his watch, that somebody in the house must know something about it, and before anybody went out of the house every man should be searched—the prisoner got up and said he would search me first—he did so, and then I searched him—I could not find it—every body was searched in the house—there were ten or twelve there, all men—the prisoner afterwards said he had three or four miles to go, and he would be there on the following night, and he left about half-past eight or nine o'clock—the watch was missed before seven o'clock—the search took place about a quarter of an hour after that—I had looked about the premises before I searched.
Cross-examined. Q. Had several of you been drinking at that shop? A. Murray had and the rest—he had no more than to be capable of taking care of himself—I searched all over the prisoner, all about his clothes and pockets—he had unbuttoned his jacket and taken it off, and I searched his trowsers.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Justice Park.
[trialAccount t18380514-1368]
1368. THOMAS WOOD was indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Thomas Wells, at Wanstead, about the hour of two in the morning of the 4th of May, with intent to steal, and steal-ing therein 2 pairs of candlesticks, value 1 l. 10 s.; 1 pair of snuffers tray, value 1 l.; 2 cruet-stands, value 1 l. 10 s.; 8 cruets, value 1 l. 1 waiter, value 1 l. 10 s.; 2 table-covers, value 1 l.; 1 pair of decantes stands, value 10 s.; and 4 sheets, value 5 s.; his goods.
ANN DICKENSON . I am servant to Mr. Thomas Wells—my husbead is also in his service—we live in his house, which is in the parish of was stead, Essex—he rents the house. On the night of Friday, the 4th of may Mr. Wells was the last person up—I went to bed at ten o'clock—I faster all the doors and windows safely myself—I left nobody up but Mr. Web—I came down at a quarter before six o'clock the next morning, as the patrol rang at the kitchen bell—I found the shutter of the dining-room forced open, which I am quite certain I had fastened—the bolt was broken off and the bar quite bent—the dining-room faces a lawn at the back the house—I missed a pair of plated candlesticks, snuffers, and tray, the dining-room, also two red table-covers, two sets of castors and stand four shells, and a plated waiter—I found on the lawn a tea-chest broken open and a hammer, which had been taken out of the table drawer, and a take lamp—everything but the shells were brought back again that day.
THOMAS WELLS . I live in the parish of Wanstead. I went to bed in mediately after the servant—I did not alter the doors or windows called me about six o'clock in the morning—I found the shutter broke I missed the articles she named, and the shells—I saw my property she afterwards at the house of Mr. Plaxton, a neighbour, whose watchman had stopped a man with them—I knew them again, and I know the shells from seeing them constantly on the mantel-piece—the value of the property is about 12 l.
PATRICK MAHONET . I am watchman to Mr. Plaxton. About a quarter past four o'clock, on Saturday morning, the 5th of May, I saw a young; lad in one of my master's fields, about four hundred yards from Mr. Wells's—I asked him what he was doing there—he said, "Nothing"—I cannot tell who it was—I do not know the prisoner—I opened the gate, and went in, and there stood a basket by him—I had some conversation with him about it—I stooped down to open it, and then he ran away—I took it to master's house—the prisoner is not the man I am certain.
JOSEPH MATTHEWS . I am watchman of Stratford Broadway, Essex. On Friday night, between the 4th and 5th of May, I saw two men, between twelve and one o'clock, about half a mile from Mr. Wells's house—I am sure the prisoner was one of them—they' were down an area, at the back of an inhabited house—I spoke to them, collared the prisoner, brought him out of the yard, and asked him what he did in the area—he said he got in there to sleep, to lie down a bit—I rang the house-bell, for some time, and the other man came to him, and said, "Slip your coat off"—he slipped one arm off his shoulder, and the other man began to knock me about—they did not knock me down, but both got away from me—I am quite sure the prisoner was one of them—I did not know him before, but saw him again the next morning, about half-past three o'clock, coming to wards London, in a straight direction from Mr. Wells's, about half a mile from it—I said, "Good morning"—he said, "Good morning, what o'clock is it?"—I said, "Half-past three"—he said, "Is it as much as that?"—I said nothing about having seen him the night before—he kept walking on, and I followed him, till I saw one of my men, named "King George"—I
held up my hand, and he stopped him, put his hand into his pocket, and pulled out four shells, and a little phial.
KING GEORGE . I am a watchman. On the Saturday morning, Matthews made a signal to me—I saw the prisoner, and stopped him, and asked him he had got any thing about him—he said, "No"—I observed him shifting his hands, as if he had got something—I searched him, and found four shells, and a phial on him—two in his coat-pocket, and two in his four shells-pocket—I asked him how he came by them—he said he bought then—I asked him who of—he said he bought them down the road, he did not know who of.
Prisoner. Q. Did not Matthews say, when you asked if he was sure I was the man who ran from him, that he was not certain? A. He said he was pretty well sure, but you were very much like the man, and he thought you were the man.
Prisoner. At Lambeth-street Office the watchman were quarrelling, and he did deny my being the man—it was not the witness, but a shorter man—there were four of them there.
THOMAS WELLS re-examined. I know these shells—I am quite sure they are mine—I am positive they were in my dining-room the day before the robbery.
ANN DICKINSON re-examined. I have the care of the room, and see the shells every morning—I am certain these are my master's.
Prisoner's Defence. On Friday night I went to the Pavilion, and being out late that night, could not get in at home, I thought I would take a walk down Stratford-road, and by the church I met a young man, who asked if I wanted to buy any shells—he wanted 18 d. for the four—I bid him 1 s., which he took.
GUILTY . Aged 21.— Transported for Life.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18380514-1369]
1369. JAMES SKIGGS and WILLIAM SALES were indicted for stealing, on the 29th of April, 3 bushels of potatoes, value 4 s. 6 d., the goods of John Scrafton Thompson.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM HOGG . I am a horse-patrol, at Stratford. On Sunday morning, the 29th of April, about five o'clock, I was in the neighbourhood of Ilford, returning from the station, in company with Johnson—near Mr. Thompson's field there was a drove of cattle in the road—I saw two men come from the horse-road down to the footpath—each of them had a bag on his shoulder, which appeared to be full—they came from among the cattle on to the footpath—we followed one of the men towards Ilford—he went up an alley which leads to the Britannia beer-shop—we followed him about fifty yards up this alley—he put down the sack, and we went up to him—it was Skiggs—I asked what he had got—he wade no reply—I opened the sack, and found it contained potatoes—I asked how he came, by them—he said he had found them on the road—he was standing at the door of a house, which was open—I gave him into Davis's custody—in consequence of some conversation with Davis, I went with him and Johnson to Sales' house—before we went into the house, we listened—I heard a conversation, but could make no sense of what was said—we knocked, and Sales opened the door—he was asked what he had made of
the bag of potatoes he had just brought in—he said he had no potatoes in his house; that he had brought none in, and had not been out of his house from the time he went to bed the night previous—this was about half-past five o'clock—we found a bag in a cupboard in the lower part of the house, similar to what I had seen the men carrying, and it contained potatoes—I cannot speak to Sales being one of the men I saw on the road.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. The men came from the cattle? A. Yes—I could not see them till they came on the footpath, near Mr. Thompson's field—when I first saw them they were just coming from the road to the footpath.
DAVID JOHNSON . I was with Hogg—I saw two men, each with a sack on his back—they appeared to me to be crossing from a gap in Mr. Thompson's fence on to the footpath on the other side—there was clamp of potatoes about 100 yards from the gap—there was a field between that and the road—I observed the impressions of two persons coming through that gap, but the mould being loose, I could not ascertain the measure of the marks—I went to Sales's house—before I went in I listened, and heard some conversation between a man and a woman.
Cross-examined. Q. Did not Sales say that a person of the name of Richard somebody had brought the sack there? A. He said then that he had no sack in the house—he said next day that a man had brought it, and went out over the pales the back way—I found no man but Sales there.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. What did you hear? A. A man said, "If it had not been for the b—patrols"—I then knocked, and Sales opened the door—I said, "Where are these potatoes?"—he said, "I have got no potatoes in my house"—he opened the back-door—I said, "No, I shall look into this cupboard," and there was the sack of potatoes—they were wet and warm, as if they had just come from the clamp.
SAMUEL DAVIS . I am a constable. I took Skiggs to the cage—I had reason to suspect who the other man was, and directed the officers to Sales—I heard him say something about the b—patrols, but I could not make out what—we went in, and Hogg asked for the potatoes—he said he had none in the house, but we found them in the cupboard, in a sack, and some were in a basket—they appeared the same sort as those in Mr. Thompson's clamp.
GABRIEL BARTHOLOMEW PAINE . I have been a farmer—I am looker to Mr. John Scrafton Thompson. I know his clamp of potatoes very well—on Saturday night, the 28th of April, it was safe, and next morning it had been broken into—I observed the gap in the fence—I have examined the two bags of potatoes that were found—I have no doubt they are the same as had been in Mr. Thompson's clamp.
SKIGGS *— GUILTY . Aged 23.— Confined One Year.
SALES *— GUILTY . Aged 39.— Transported for Seven Years.

1370. JOHN GILSON and EDWARD GILSON were indicted for stealing, on the 28th of March, 115lbs. weight of lead, value 20 s., the goods of Henry Gunson, and fixed to a building.
HENRY GUNSON . I live at West Ham. On the 25th of March I saw the lead safe on my summer-house, and on the 28th I missed it—I have compared this lead with the place it was taken from, and it corresponds in every respect—( looking at it.)
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did you compare it since you
were before the Magistrate? A. No, before—I believe I told the Magistrate I had done so, but I am not certain—I identified it.
PETER HILL . I am a gardener. About six o'clock in the morning of at 28th of March, I met the two prisoners coming up the side of the field—they had these two rolls of lead—each of them had one under his arm—I asked where they got it from—they said from their uncle—they left the lead in the road, right opposite Mr. Gunson's lodge, and walked away—I am sure the prisoners are the persons.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you known them before? A. I had seen them—I did not know them by name—I never spoke to them, or knew where they lived—I had seen them in a gentleman's garden, where I used to work—they got over the fence after the apples.
JOHN ELSON . I am a constable of Plaistow. I compared this lead with the summer-house, and it corresponded exactly.
WILLIAM GIBLING . I took the prisoner on the 26th of April. One was in prison then.
JOHN GIBSON *— GUILTY . Aged 20.
EDWARD GIBSON *— GUILTY . Aged 17.

1372. JOHN CHARLTON was indicted for stealing, on the 21st of April, 1 watch, value 5 l., the goods of Elizabeth Sinnock Bennett, his mistress.
JOHN BENNETT . I am a watchmaker and jeweller, and live in Stockwell-street,
Greenwich, with my mother, Elizabeth Sinnock Bennett. A gold watch belonging to Mr. Flower, of Lea, was left in my mother's care to be repaired—it was put in the workshop till it should be applied for—the officer afterwards produced it—this is the same watch—( looking at it.)
JAMES WHISKARD . I am a pawnbroker, and live in Bishopsgate-street. On the afternoon of the 21st of April, the prisoner brought this watch to pledge—I asked him whose it was—he said his own—I asked him where he got it from—he said his father gave it to him—I said, "Where does he live?"—he said, "No. 16, Worship-street"—I could see by his manner he had not got possession of the watch as he should have done, and I called in a policeman, and sent him to where he said his father lived—he brought him back, and said it was all wrong.
JAMES HALL ( City police-constable 14.) I took the prisoner into custody at Mr. Whiskard's shop—he said he wanted to go to No. 16, Worship-street, where his father lived—I took him there, and showed him the house—I said, "Shall I knock at the door, is this the house?"—he said, "No"—I looked at him, and he began to cry—I said, "Is it all wrong?"—he said, "Yes, sir"—I took him back to Mr. Whiskard's, and at the station-house I found on him a cheque for 21 l., a silver watch, and a metal watch, which I produce, and a few shillings.
WILLIAM HOUGH . I am a watchmaker in Mrs. Bennett's employ. This gold watch is hers—it was in her care to repair—the prisoner was our errand boy, and was left in the workshop at different times while we were absent—he was intrusted with this silver watch to bring up to town—I know the metal watch to be Mrs. Bennett's property, but when it was taken I cannot say.
JOHN BENNETT re-examined. He was one of two errand boys—the other is in prison now for robbing us—they had both been in the house some time together.
Prisoner. I acknowledge taking the gold watch—I took it on the saturday morning that I was taken into custody—I was sent to Tottenham to pay a cheque, and not having any money with me I took the watch to pawn to pay for my coach-hire—I meant to take it out again on the Monday, and replace it. Witness. He was supplied with 11 s. to pay his expenses, and that was taken from his pocket—he was intrusted to take the cheque to Tottenham—he had still got that about him.
GUILTY . Aged 16.— Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18380514-1373]
1373. HENRY KNIGHT was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of April, 1 half-crown, 7 shillings, 6 sixpences, and 3 fourpenny pieces, the monies of William Covell, his master.
JAMES COVELL . I am the son of William Covell, a butcher, in Broadway, Deptford. The prisoner was in his employ—in consequence of suspicions I entertained on the 17th of April, I marked one half-crown, seven shillings, six sixpences, and three fourpenny pieces, and put them into the till—I examined the till the following morning, and found the marked money still there—I then put it into a purse into a desk in the shop, and afterwards missed the money from the desk, but not the purse—I sent for a policeman—the prisoner was searched—one marked shilling was found on him, and another the policeman got from the maid servant—I had left the key of the desk on the mantel-shelf in the parlour, and the desk was in the shop.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Is your father here to-day? A. No—this prosecution is with his consent—quite so—he has not said he had rather have paid 402. than the prisoner should be prosecuted—he said he would rather pay 40 l. than he should be transported—I have not had any particular quarrel with the prisoner—I do not remember that I quarrelled with him the day he was taken into custody—I will not swear I did not—it was on Thursday evening, the 26th, he was apprehended—I had put the marked money into the desk on the Wednesday week previous—it was always the custom to leave the key of that desk on the mantel-shelf when I went to work, before my father was up—it was not left unlocked—the money remained untouched for a week—all the family and the maid servant had access to the room where the key was—I was not playing at a game called cock-shy with the prisoner on the day he was taken—I saw him and George Munns playing at it in my father's yard—I do not remember quarrelling with him then—I did not play with him at all that day—I did the day before—at least I was playing with Munns, and he wished to play with us— cock-shy is a stick stuck in the ground with a piece of money on the top of it to throw at—I see you understand it—I had some shillings the day before, but none passed between us—I changed some sixpences with him, but nothing else—I do not think any passed between Munns and me—my father desired me to send for a policeman—he was at home at the time.
Q. Had you paid the prisoner any silver between the time of your depositing the money in the desk, and your causing him to be taken into custody? A. Yes, 11 s. 6 d.—most of that was in sixpences and four-penny pieces—I cannot tell what the rest was—I cannot say whether there were any shillings—I paid it him on the Sunday morning previous to his being taken—I will swear I did not pay him as many as eleven shillings—I had not marked any other money before—it was about eleven o'clock on Thursday night, that I found the marked money gone from the desk—it was about one o'clock in the afternoon that the prisoner and Munns were playing at cock-shy —the prisoner lost two shillings to me the day before, and two shillings to Munns—he continued to play with Munns and I left them—I did not go after that, and play at skittles at the Druid's Head—I had been there on the Monday before with Munns, and the prisoner wished to go to stick up the skittles—I scarcely ever played before, and should not then had it not been for my cousin Munns being in town—I am not in the habit of frequenting skittle-grounds—I will swear I did not play at cock-shy with the prisoner on the Thursday that he was taken—I wished the prisoner to go about his business, because I wished the business to be done—there were some sheep to be killed, and I wished him to go and do it—( my father was at home at the time)—he said he wished to have a game first—I told him to go and kill the sheep—nothing else passed—we did not quarrel—I think they left off playing in my presence—it was about three o'clock in the afternoon—not so late as five or six o'clock—the prisoner came to live with my father through a friend—he was not engaged because I was in the habit of playing at skittles and cock-shy, and would not attend to the business.
ELIZABETH HOATH . I am in the prosecutor's service. The prisoner borrowed eighteen-pence of me about two months ago, and on the 26th of April he paid me one shilling of it back—he said, he had not got a sixpence—I put the shilling into my pocket, and gave it to the officer
afterwards, having first marked it—the prisoner paid it me of his own accord—I did not ask for it.
JOHN ROSCOE ( police-constable R 119.) I searched the prisoner and found a marked shilling in his trowsers pocket—I showed it to James Covill, and he said it was a shilling which he had marked—I also received another marked shilling from Hoath, and she marked it in my presence—these are them.
Cross-examined. Q. Who sent for you? A. One of Mr. Covill's sons, but not James—he gave the prisoner in charge—he said he supposed he had been robbing the desk of the marked money.
JAMES COVILL re-examined. A marked shilling was also found on Munns—these are two of the shillings I marked—the money I paid him on the Sunday I got from a different desk.
GEORGE MUNNS . I had a marked shilling among my other money—I do not know how I got it—I had received a shilling from the prisoner in the course of Thursday—I won 1 s. of him—there was no quarrel between my cousin and the prisoner in my presence.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you been playing with your cousin as well as the prisoner? A. Not that day—I did the day before—I had been in town about a week—I do not know bow many times we played at this game—we had nothing else to do to amuse ourselves—I had not won any money of my cousin, nor he of me—I cannot say whether he lost anything to the prisoner—we took turns in putting the money on the sticks—I think my cousin put sixpence on—I played at skittles once at the Druid's Head with my cousin—I do not remember who won there—I was at one other place with the prisoner—I never had more than 1 s. from the prisoner—I never had any money from my cousin on any occasion—I do not remember whether I knocked any money off which he put on the stick.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Common Sergeant
[trialAccount t18380514-1374]
1374. ELIZABETH EDDS was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of April, 1 shawl, value 2 s. 6 d.; 3 shillings, 4 pence, and 16 halfpence; the goods and monies of Mary Mahoney; and that she had been before convicted of felony.
MARY MAHONEY . I am a widow, and live in Harding-lane, Woolwich. On the 9th of April I went up stairs to put my child to bed, and left my shawl and this money down in my room—the prisoner lives next door to me—I came down in ten minutes, and my shawl and money were gone—it was between six and seven o'clock in the evening—this is my shawl, I am certain—( looking at it.)
Prisoner. Q. Did you not lend me a shawl that morning? A. No, I never had any thing to do with you—I had only lived there seven days.
FRANCIS CHANDLER . I am shopman to Mr. Davis, a pawnbroker at Woolwich. On the evening of the 19th of April, Millar pawned this shawl with me.
PHILIP MILLAR . I am a baker. I was at the Duchess of Wellington public-house—the prisoner called me outside, and asked if I would take the shawl and pawn it—I said yes, and did so—she gave it to me off her shoulders.
Prisoner's Defence. In the morning I asked the prosecutrix to lend me a shawl, to go down the town, which she did; and when I came home, she said she wanted it, and I might have the other if I liked—in the evening I asked that young man to get 9 d. on it, and I would fetch it out in the morning; and when I was apprehended I told them I would fetch it—I had pawned it for 9 d.
CHARLES STEWART WARDEN . I am superintendent of the watch at Woolwich. I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction of felony from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—the prisoner is the woman.
GUILTY . Aged 49.— Transported for Seven Years.

1375. FRANCES HOPKINS was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of April, 1 shift, value 2 s.; 1 night-gown, value 2 s.; 1 sheet, value 2 s.; 1 shawl, value 2 s.; 1 petticoat, value 1 s. 6 d.; and 1 pair of stockings, value 6 d.; the goods of Richard Gilbert, her master.
SARAH GILBERT . I am the wife of Richard Gilbert, and live in the Lewisham-road, in the parish of Deptford. The prisoner was in my service—she left us on the morning of the 5th of April, and I missed the articles stated in the indictment, which are here,—they are ours—the was tent by the parish to nurse me.
Prisoner. You lent me the things, or I never should have taken them—you wanted me to come—I was very ill, and I had not time to bring my own clothes—you said you would lend me the things. Witness. On my oath, it is not true.
Prisoner. It was a roguish business of mine, certainly; but I have been used to good service, and had not she been a rogue of a mistreat I never should have run away.
JOHN DYKE . I am a policeman. I was on duty on this morning, and saw the prisoner, about ten or eleven o'clock, in Trafalgar-row, Greenwich, with this parcel in her hand—I took it from her—it was part of the property stolen.

1376. CHARLOTTE DEBNAM was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of May, 5 pillows, value 10 s.; 4 blankets, value 1 l. 3 s.; 10 yards of carpet, value 55.; 1 table-cloth, value 2 s.; 1 bolster, value 2 s.; 1 wine-glass, value 6 d.; and I saw, value 2 s.; the goods of Samuel Tunnicliff.
ELIZABETH TUNNICLIFF . I am the wife of Samuel Tunnicliff—he keeps a public-house at Deptford. The prisoner lodged at my house—I missed the articles named in the indictment, some of which are here—when she went away some duplicates were found, which I gave to the officer.
BENJAMIN HASELDINE . I am a pawnbroker. I produce the property—I cannot tell who pawned it, but I have the corresponding duplicates to those produced.
BENJAMIN LOVELL . I took the prisoner into custody, and told her the charge—she said she intended to pay Mr. Tunnicliff, and get the things out.
Prisoner's Defence. I had no felonious intention—the prosecutrix compromised the matter by taking 10 s., and retained the duplicates in her possession—my husband paid her the 10 s., and told her to make her election either to prosecute or take part of the money.
ELIZABETH TUNNICLIFF re-examined. It is not so—she owed me 5 l. for rent, and I took the 10 s. for that—I gave the husband a receipt for it—I found the duplicates under the bed after she was gone.
GUILTY . Aged 41.— Confined Six Months.
Before Mr. Justice Park.

1378. JOHN KNOTT was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of April 1 cloak, value 18 s.; and 1 shawl, value 2 s.; the goods of William Smith: and 1 cloak, value 10 s.; the goods of Mary Griffiths.
WILLIAM SMITH . I was on a visit at Mr. Mason's, at Deptford. On the 25th of April an alarm was given by my wife's sister that the window was open—I ran out, and on going down Grove-street I saw two young met—I made towards them—to the best of my belief the prisoner was one of them—they divided—one came towards me without a bundle—the prisoner went on with the bundle—I walked after him—he walked fast, I began to run—he dropped the bundle and ran on—a man took him—I picked up the bundle, and it contained these two cloaks and the shawl—two of them are my wife's—I will not be positive the prisoner is the lad who dropped the bundle, but I believe he is.
GEORGE GRIFFITHS . I live at Deptford. I saw the prosecutor running after the prisoner, crying "Stop thief,"—he dropped this bundle close by me—I am certain he is the person.
FRANCIS WALL . I saw the prisoner running towards the Plough-bridge—he ran and concealed himself under the palings.
Prisoner's Defence. He did not see me running. I did not have the bundle at all—the witness said he could not swear to me at all.
GEORGE GRIFFITHS re-examined. I stared in his face—I saw the prosecutor running—the prisoner walked gently past me till he got four or five yards, and then ran off directly.
GUILTY . Aged 17.— Confined Six Months.

1380. CHARLES CHAPMAN was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of April, 1 watch-ribbon, value 1 s.; 1 seal, value 6 d.; 1 watch-key, value 6 d.; and 2 split-rings, value 6 d.; the goods of Samuel Ind, from his person.
SAMUEL IND . I live in Devonshire-terrace, Devonshire-street, St. Dunstan's. On the 28th of April I was crossing Greenwich-park towards Blackheath—the prisoner came up to me and asked the time—I said, "Twenty-five minutes to one"—I had a watch with a ribbon and key—I took it out to look, and I put it into my fob—he turned round twice—I suspected he meant no good, and I turned to the road—he turned and said again, "What time is it?"—I told him again, and he snatched at my guard—it broke, and then he snatched at my ribbon, and took it with the key, and two rings—he went towards the gate of the park—I followed him and had him taken, and they were found on him.
AARON OUTRED ( police-constable R 128.) I heard the cry of "Stop thief"—I went up and took the prisoner—I found a ribbon, a key, and two rings in his pocket, which the prosecutor claimed.
GUILTY . Aged 40.— Transported for Ten Years.
( There was another indictment against the prisoner.)

1381. RICHARD FOWLER was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of April, 3 razors, value 5 s.; and 1 front of hair, value 1 s.; the goods of William Moore.
WILLIAM MOORE . I am a hair-dresser. On the 30th of April the prisoner came to me, with another man, who wanted his hair cut, and while I was doing it the prisoner was very busy about the shop, looking about—the other man got up, with his hair half cut, and said, "I won't have any more of this;" and they both went out without paying me—about three minutes after they were gone I missed three razors and a hair front—these are them—( looking at them.)
Prisoner. Q. Which man went out first? A. You went out first—I asked you to sit down, but you would not—you went to the glass and combed your hair out—you have come into my shop before you washed.
GEORGE HOGG . Between five and six o'clock, in the evening of the 30th of April, I was in the tap-room of the King's Arms, and bought these two razors of the prisoner.
Prisoner. Q. Was there not another man with me? A. There was not.
GUILTY . Aged 23.— Confined Three Months.

1385. JOHN WILKINS and JAMES PAYNE were indicted for stealing, on the 16th of April, 1 handkerchief, value 5 s.; the goods of Joseph Groves, from his person.
JOSEPH GROVES . On the 16th of April I was at Greenwich-fair. I had a handkerchief in my coat-pocket—I did not miss it till the policeman told me of it—I then followed, and saw him take it from the breeches of one of the prisoners—this is it.
BENJAMIN SAMUEL BLABEN ( police-constable A 56.) About half-past five o'clock on the 16th of April, I saw the two prisoners in Greenwich-fair, trying several persons' pockets—they were together, and sometimes armin-arm in front of Richardson's show—they were lifting up pockets and putting their hands in—when they got to the prosecutor, Payne took a silk handkerchief from his pocket, and passed it to Wilkins, who was covering him as he did it—I told the prosecutor and took Payne—Brooks took Wilkins.
JAMES BROOKS ( police-constable L 118.) I took the handkerchief from Wilkins' breeches—what Blaben has said is true.
Wilkins' Defence. I was standing in front of the show when some person, I do not know who, thrust a handkerchief into my hand, and I thrust it into my button-hole, having no pockets.
( The prisoners received excellent characters.)
WILKINS— GUILTY . Aged 15.
PAYNE— GUILTY . Aged 15.
Recommended to mercy— Confined Three Months .
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

1386. CHARLES MARTIN was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of April, one handkerchief, value 6 d.; the goods of Robert Chapman, from his person.

ROBERT CLARKE ( police-constable R 167.) [trialAccount t18380514-1387]
1387. MARTHA WEARING was indicted for embezzlement.
REV. JOHN SMALLMAN MASTERS . I live at Greenwich—the prisoner was employed as collectress to the Greenwich Provident Society, of which I am treasurer—myself and others subscribed to the society—the prisoner was servant to me and the society in 1836—it was her duty to receive deposits, and pay them to me—if she received 1 s. on the 10th of April, 1838, she has not paid it to me.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Was there any document in writing, of the society and the persons who formed the subscribers and trustees? A. Yes; but I have it not with me—the prisoner is charged with receiving 2 s. from Lygo in April last year—the Provident Society was dissolved in December, 1836, previous to the receipt of this shilling—the prisoner was not a servant of the society after December, 1836—in August, 1837, she asked me for her salary as collectress of the society—I laughed at her, and told her she was no servant of the society—I believe she told me then that not knowing the society was dissolved in December, she had collected some monies, and I told her to return them to the parties.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18380514-1388]
1388. MARTHA WEARING was again indicted for obtaining money by false pretences.
REV. JOHN SMALLMAN MASTERS . I was treasurer to the Provident Society, to which others subscribed up to December, 1836—it was then dissolved—the prisoner was our servant from the 17th of May, 1836, to the end of December—she was to receive monies and collect subscriptions during that period—she had not a written authority, but a verbal authority, but not after 1836—the society was at an end then—I do not know whether she knew that it was dissolved—she had no notice that she was discharged from me—we took it for granted that she knew it was dissolved—in August she applied to me for her salary, and said she had received some monies,—I
told her she had no right to receive them, and to take them back again to the parties.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18380514-1389]
1389. EDWARD GEORGE was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MESSRS. BODKIN and ESPINASSE conducted the Prosecution.
JOSEPH WOOD . I am thirteen years old. I live in Rhode-street Greenwich, with Allen, a police-constable—on the 7th of April I saw the prisoner between eight and nine o'clock in the evening, in Rhode-street—he asked me to go into Mr. Moore's shop, to get him a quarter of an ounce of tobacco, and he gave me a sixpence to pay for it—I got the tobaca, and gave Mrs. Moore the sixpence—she gave me change—I did not see where she put the sixpence—I went to the prisoner and gave him the change, and he ran away—he had promised me a halfpenny for my trouble before I went in, but he did not give it me.
Prisoner. Q. Was there any body with me? A. No—I gave you 5 1/4 d. change.
AMELIA MOORE . My husband's name is George Moore. He is a beer-seller in Rhode-street, Greenwich—I saw Wood on the 7th of April, at near nine o'clock in the evening—I knew him before—he came for a quarter of an ounce of tobacco—it came to 3/4 d.—he gave me a 6 d.—I gave him change—I took the sixpence up, and found it was bad—I gave it to my husband to examine, and he went out with it to see for the little boy—I got the sixpence from my husband, and put it on a shelf by itself—I afterwards marked it, and gave it to the policeman.
GEORGE MOORE . I am the husband of Amelia Moore. I received a sixpence from her on the 7th of April, between eight and nine o'clock in the evening—I looked at it, and said it was a bad one—I went to the uncle of Joseph Wood, and I gave the sixpence to one Fisher—he gave it me back, and I gave it my wife—she put it on a shelf—nobody but me my wife has access to that shelf.
Prisoner. Q. You said you took it to the boy's mother? A. No, he has no mother—it was not out of my hand.
MARY ANN BEDWELL . I am the daughter of James Bed well; he lives in Thames-street, Greenwich. I saw the prisoner on the 10th of April—he asked me where, they sold tobacco—I told him, in Mr. Barnes's shop—he said that would not do—he then gave me a sixpence, and told me to go and get a pennyworth of tobacco—I went to Mr. Home's shop, and asked for a pennyworth of tobacco—I got it, and gave the sixpence which the prisoner gave me—I got the change, and I went back to the prisoner—I gave him the change and the tobacco—he gave me another sixpence to get a penny candle—he did not tell me where to go—I went to Mr. Waller's, and got a candle, and gave the sixpence for it, which he gave me—I got the change and the candle—I gave the change to the prisoner—he gave me the candle for my trouble.
Prisoner. The woman said she had not served anybody, and she showed the policeman the sixpences she had—he said there was not one bad.
WILLIAM THOMAS HORNE . My father's name is John Home; he lives in Thames-street, Greenwich. On Tuesday evening, the 10th of April, I saw the prisoner between eight and nine o'clock, in Thames-street—he asked me to go for an egg for him—he gave me a bad sixpence—I paid
that sixpence at Mrs. Waller's—she gave me 5 1/4 d. which I gave to the prisoner, and he went away.
Prisoner. Q. When you went into that shop, Mrs. Waller asked you who sent you with the sixpence, and you said your mother? A. Yes—told me to say it was for my mother.
MARY WALLER . I am the wife of Jonathan Sherman Waller, a chandler and shoemaker, in Thames-street, Greenwich. On the 10th of April, between eight and nine o'clock in the evening, Mary Ann Bedwell came for a penny candle—she paid me a sixpence, and I gave her 5 d. change—I put the sixpence on the shelf—there was nothing with it—in two or three minutes afterwards, Home came for a penny egg—I said I had not got one—I gave him a three-farthing one—he paid for it with a sixpence—I gave him 5 1/4 d. change—I sent my little girl to fetch her father, and gave him both the sixpences together.
JONATHAN SHERMAN WALLER . On the 10th of April, I received two sixpences from my wife, between eight and nine o'clock in the evening—I sent one double, and went up the street—I told the policeman, there were some men passing bad money—I went back to my shop with him, and marked the sixpences, and gave them to him.
THOMAS HICKLINO ( police-constable R 118.) I received two sixpences from Mr. Jonathan Sherman Waller—these are them—( producing them)—I received one sixpence from Mrs. Moore—this is it—( producing another)—I took the prisoner into custody—I found on him one farthing—that is all.
MR. JOHN FIELD . I am inspector of coin to the Mint—these three sixpences are all counterfeit, and two of them were cast in the tame mould—the other is not.
Prisoner's Defence. I was at home at the time the children swear I gave them the sixpences—I buy and tell fish about the town—when the policeman came to me, I went with him—the Inspector would not book the charge—he told me to come next morning, and the policeman came to me, and I went—it is not likely that I should go about the town, where I get my living, and pass bad money.
GUILTY .*—Aged 21. Confined Two Years.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18380514-1390]
1390. WILLIAM ELLIS and WILLIAM CALLY were indicted for stealing, on the 16th of April, 1 handkerchief, value 1 s., the goods of Charles Graham Marshall, from his person.
GEORGE THORNTON ( police-constable E 91.) On the 16th of April, I was on duty in Greenwich fair—I saw the prisoners, about six o'clock in the evening, trying several gentlemen's pockets—I was with another constable—I saw Cally take from the prosecutor this silk handkerchief, and hand it to Ellis—he was about putting it into his breast, when Dowsing took him, and I took Cally—I spoke to the prosecutor, who identified it—he stated before the Magistrate that his name was Charles Graham Marshall, and signed the charge as such—he goes by that name at his lodging—he signed his name on the sheet, "Charles Graham Marshall," and answered to that name—I searched Cally at the station-house, and found another handkerchief round his leg, and a pair of gloves in his pocket—I called at the prosecutor's lodgings, and find he is gone to France, and not expected to return for three years.
THOMAS DOWSING . I searched the prisoner Ellis, and found on him a fish-hook, with which they generally take handkerchiefs, and a snuff-box some cigars, and a lucifer match-box."
Ellis's Defence. It is my mother's box—I am in Bishopsgate work ho se—she sent me out to get some snuff—I put the box into my pocket and went to the fair.
(Cally received a good character.)
ELLIS— GUILTY . Aged 15.
CALLY— GUILTY . Aged 14.
Recommended to mercy by the Jury. Confined Three Months.
SURREY CASES.
Before Mr. Recorder.
